<summary><strong>原文 / 抓取到的正文</strong></summary>

U.S. crude oil tops $100 as Iran escalates Hormuz attacks
At least nine commercial ships have come under attack near Hormuz since Sunday as Iran tries to choke off crude oil exports through the strait.
The "energy ceasefire" that President Donald Trump tried to broker between Ukraine and Russia has quickly unraveled.
Iran allied Houthi militants in Yemen have repeatedly fired missiles and drones at Saudi Arabia this week.
Rapidan Energy’s McNally: Three conflicts are impacting energy flows and fixed energy infrastructure
Crude oil prices rose sharply Friday as Iran escalated attacks in the Strait of Hormuz, Houthi militants fired missiles at Saudi Arabia and Ukraine vowed to keep striking Russian refineries.
Brent futures hit a session high of $110 per barrel for the first time since May before easing slightly to close at $109.86, a gain of 3.7%.
U.S. West Texas Intermediate rose 3.5% to settle at $101.41 per barrel. WTI added more than 10% this week while Brent advanced nearly 5%.
Saudi Arabia intercepted several missiles fired over the Red Sea on Friday, including one that was heading toward the oil export terminal Yanbu, a military spokesman said. Three drones were also destroyed in the kingdom&#x27;s Riyadh and southern regions, the military said.
Iran-allied Houthi militants in Yemen have repeatedly fired at Saudi Arabia this week. Oil giant Aramco came under attack when one of its administrative buildings in Dhahran was hit Tuesday, the kingdom&#x27;s energy ministry said .
The Houthis attacked Riyadh&#x27;s international airport on Oct. 10, killing 12 and injuring more than 300.
In the Strait of Hormuz, at least nine tankers and cargo ships have come under attack since Sunday as Iran tries to choke off oil exports through the waterway. Iran sank the container ship GFS Pride on Wednesday, U.S. Central Command said. The crew of the Singapore-registered ship was safely evacuated, according to Singapore&#x27;s maritime authority.
The attacks have left three seafarers injured and two missing this week, according to the International Maritime Organization .
Crude exports through Hormuz have pulled back after touching prewar levels in September. Shipments stood at 9.4 million barrels per day for the week ended Wednesday, about 70% of the average before the Iran war, according to data provided by the energy researcher Kpler.
Meanwhile, the " energy ceasefire " that President Donald Trump tried to broker between Ukraine and Russia has quickly unraveled. The Kremlin continued attacking Ukrainian infrastructure despite the supposed truce. President Volodymyr Zelenskyy said Kyiv would resume strikes on Russian refineries in retaliation.
Diesel prices have surged this year due to Ukraine&#x27;s attacks on Russian refineries and Iranian strikes on energy infrastructure in the Middle East. The fuel cost an average of $6.33 a gallon in the U.S. Friday, more than 70% above the same period last year, according to AAA data.
U.S. crude oil prices were also driven higher by a big decline of more than 8 million barrels for the week ended Oct. 9 in commercial inventories due to production shut-ins on the Gulf Coast from Hurricane Isaias, according to the Energy Information Administration.
Choose CNBC as your preferred source on Google and never miss a moment from the most trusted name in business news.

### 2. ★★★★☆ No 10 leaves option open to impose Chinese EV tariffs if UK joins EU scheme

<details>
<summary><strong>原文 / 抓取到的正文</strong></summary>

An electric vehicle production line in Ningbo, China. Photograph: Nick Carey/Reuters
An electric vehicle production line in Ningbo, China. Photograph: Nick Carey/Reuters
No 10 leaves option open to impose Chinese EV tariffs if UK joins EU scheme
Beijing warns against ‘discriminatory’ step after France drops opposition to British role in Made in Europe package
No 10 has left the door open to imposing tariffs on Chinese-made electric vehicles as part of the Made in Europe package, prompting a warning from Beijing that such a move would be “discriminatory”.
Downing Street said on Friday that it would “welcome the objective to have the UK included in the Industrial Accelerator Act” after France dropped its opposition to Britain joining the scheme .
The aim of the rules is to curb subsidised Chinese imports by giving EU-made products preferential treatment, but the UK and others such as Norway are worried about being left at a disadvantage if excluded from the scheme.
The price for joining would be aligning with the EU’s tariffs on Chinese EVs imposed by the bloc in 2024, according to a Franco-German proposal on Thursday night that said the UK would have to dynamically align with EU trade defence measures.
The Chinese embassy in London said it was “firmly opposed to any discriminatory practice involving tariff hikes or restrictive measures on Chinese products”.
“We have expressed serious concern to the UK side regarding the reports in question,” it said, adding that “trade protectionism runs counter to WTO rules and market principles”.
This week, the business secretary, Jonathan Reynolds, said he would not be coerced into aligning with the EU’s policy.
However, the government has been desperate to be included in the Made in Europe package, fearing billions of pounds of trade in the car industry could be lost if the UK is locked out of supply chains in the EU.
Asked whether the UK accepted it would have to put tariffs on Chinese-made vehicles in order to join Made in Europe, a No 10 spokesperson said it was “premature to speculate” on the terms of any deal with the bloc and there was “still a European process to run”.
They added: “But more broadly, on the principle, the UK obviously has its own trade policy and it keeps all tariffs under review. And we work closely with the British automotive sector to make sure that that approach reflects their best interests.”
Chinese EVs are increasingly popular, with BYD sales up 78% year on year in September, Chery up 660% and the lesser-known Changan up by more than 3,000% in terms of market share.
EU lawmakers are expected to finalise the Industrial Accelerator Act, known as Made in Europe legislation, by the end of the year or early 2027.
But MEPs have already tabled 5,100 amendments, including one embracing the UK and giving Andy Burnham time to secure the deal he wants.
Earlier this week the boss of Mercedes-Benz, Ola Källenius, who is also the president of the European car trade body ACEA, warned that locking the UK out of legislation would amount to a “deliberate choice” by EU leaders to “hurt” the car industry across the bloc.
According to the new Franco-German proposal, there would be “an opt-in to further include European partners who were part of the EU single market as of 1 January 2020”, which includes the UK and Norway.
This would be provided those third countries accepted “dynamic alignment on the EU’s conditions related to third countries’ investments and on relevant EU trade defence measures”.
Tariffs could help UK-based manufacturers by preventing cut-price Chinese models flooding the market but they could also push up prices for consumers and harm British manufacturers trying to sell vehicles to China in the case of an escalating trade war. About 80% of UK-made cars are exported, with more than half going to the EU and 6% to China.
Earlier on Thursday the trade minister, Anas Sarwar, hinted that the UK was minded to align on tariffs. He told Politico the UK and the EU must “work together and act in lockstep” to tackle China’s dominance in the automotive sector.
A car industry source said the EU “has recently shown that it is possible to have a dialogue with the Chinese and get some form of safeguarding in place” without retaliation.
China opposes the expansion of EV tariffs. In the past few months it has made what European Commission officials called “bloodcurdling threats” to launch a trade war over the latest plan to impose quotas on Chinese hybrid cars.
While quotas can be introduced within months under World Trade Organization rules, it could take more than a year to impose tariffs on Chinese cars, with strict processes to be followed, starting with notification of an investigation and followed by evidence-gathering and sharing over alleged harms China might be causing to the British car industry.
Liam Byrne, the Labour chair of the parliamentary business committee, has said imposing tariffs on Chinese EVs would be “a small price to pay” to save the industry in both the UK and the EU.
At a conference in London on Thursday night, he said the UK “urgently needs” a new strategy on China to ensure that British and European manufacturing “does not die”.
Byrne, who has been leading an investigation into the UK’s position on China, was highly critical of the government’s approach, which he believes is naive and based on false hopes that China will change its export policy.
“Our China strategy is simply not where it needs to be. The UK urgently needs a coherent China strategy, which it does not have today,” he said.

### 3. ★★★★☆ Ship attack reports near Hormuz reach record high as oil exports slow

- **分类**: 🛢️ 能源
- **来源**: BBC World / BBC Business
- **多源确认**: 2
- **时间**: 2026-10-16T05:00:17+00:00
- **发生了什么**: BBC Verify analysis of data from a UK maritime agency shows 12 vessels were struck in a week.

<details>
<summary><strong>原文 / 抓取到的正文</strong></summary>

By Joshua Cheetham ,Â  Thomas Copeland Â  and Â  Alison Benjamin ,Â  BBC Verify
The number of ships that have reported being attacked in and around the Strait of Hormuz has hit a record high, BBC Verify analysis of data from a UK maritime agency shows.
Twelve vessels were attacked between 5 and 11 October - the largest number of weekly strikes reported to the UK&#x27;s Maritime Trade Operations (UKMTO) agency since the US-Israeli war with Iran began. The previous week saw 10 ships reporting attacks.
Six of the vessels were struck last Sunday, according to the UKMTO - a one-day high. So far this week, there have been four reported attacks.
As the number of strikes has risen, the recovery in oil shipments through the strait seen last month has slowed, industry data suggests.
BBC Verify collated incident reports published by the UKMTO on its website and created a timeline based on the first date cited in each report. This is approximate because some incidents use the date of the attack and some the date on which it was reported.
Experts say Tehran has been attacking ships in an effort to assert greater control over the waterway as the US military has increased the number of tankers it is shepherding through the south of the strait to evade Iranian strikes.
Iran is also carrying out more attacks on vessels further away from the Strait of Hormuz, according to experts.
The US military&#x27;s Central Command, which oversees operations in the Middle East, posted on social media that despite the attacks "commercial traffic continues to flow through the Strait of Hormuz with co-ordinated protection from US forces and regional partners".
Two crew missing after one of Sunday&#x27;s attacks
The aftermath of one of Sunday&#x27;s six reported attacks was captured in verified footage showing the Nicaragua-flagged tanker Petro Dolphin on fire, as filmed by the crew on a smaller boat nearby.
The vessel was struck by a drone close to the Sohar Anchorage, Oman, more than 250km (150 miles) from the strait, according to the maritime intelligence firm Vanguard.
Eleven Indian seafarers were rescued but two are still missing, the Forward Seamen&#x27;s Union of India said.
Attacks have continued this week with the container ship GFS Pride sinking after a strike by an unknown projectile in the strait on Tuesday. Twenty-one crew were safely evacuated.
Iran&#x27;s Islamic Revolutionary Guard Corps (IRGC) published footage of what appeared to be the same ship sinking. It said the vessel was carrying "2,500 containers of contraband" and had taken an "unauthorised route".
Middle East oil exports have slowed as the number of ship strikes has risen.
About 12.6 million barrels per day went through the strait on 13 October, below the 15 million reported at the end of September, data from trade intelligence firm Kpler shows. This compares with a pre-war baseline of some 17 million barrels per day.
The recent increase in attacks comes as US Navy data indicates the number of vessels given assistance to navigate the Strait of Hormuz during the first 10 days of October was up 85% compared with the same period in August.
Most of the oil being shipped from Gulf Arab states recently has been carried using a "shuttle" system where a fleet of tankers load up and sail through the strait before transferring their cargo to larger tankers in the Gulf of Oman.
These shuttle ships use a route close to Oman&#x27;s coastline. Iran says this is "unauthorised" and insist vessels take an alternative route closer to its own shore.
So-called assisted transits were a key factor in helping drive oil flows from the Middle East back towards pre-war levels in September. This does not include Iranian exports which have completely stalled as a result of the US blockade of Iranian ports and ships.
Martin Kelly, CEO of Orbital Risk Intelligence, said Iran has "evolved its strategy" in response to this loosening of its control over the strait and has been carrying out "near daily or multiple attacks every single day".
"The aim here is to stop the ships going through and to deter others," he added.
The IRGC&#x27;s navy has said strikes would not be "limited to the Strait of Hormuz" and later announced a "new deployment formation" across the region, according to state media.
Martin Kelly told BBC Verify that Iran is widening its attacks and targeting ships near the coast of Gulf Arab states and in the Gulf of Oman.
A tanker was struck by multiple projectiles north of Qatar on Thursday 8 October, more than 500km (310 miles) from the strait, resulting in multiple reported casualties, according to Vanguard.
Another tanker was attacked close to the UAE town of Al-Jazirah al-Hamra on Friday 9 October, Vanguard said.
Verified footage shows crew members running across the deck and putting on lifejackets as a large cloud of black smoke rises over the ship.

</details>

### 5. ★★★☆☆ US Stock Futures Edge Higher as Fed Chair Warsh Avoids Rate Guidance and Tesla Unveils New Roadster: Dow Jones, S&P, Nasdaq, Wall Street - Yahoo Finance

- **分类**: 📈 市场
- **来源**: GN Markets
- **多源确认**: 1
- **时间**: 2026-10-16T09:12:40+00:00
- **发生了什么**: US Stock Futures Edge Higher as Fed Chair Warsh Avoids Rate Guidance and Tesla Unveils New Roadster: Dow Jones, S&amp;P, Nasdaq, Wall Street&nbsp;&nbsp;Yahoo Finance

<details>
<summary><strong>原文 / 抓取到的正文</strong></summary>

US Stock Futures Edge Higher as Fed Chair Warsh Avoids Rate Guidance and Tesla Unveils New Roadster: Dow Jones, S&P, Nasdaq, Wall Street
Wall Street subway station sign ©Rawpixel US stock futures traded slightly higher on Friday, 16 October 2026, following gains on Wall Street in the previous session as technology shares recovered from selling pressure linked to concerns about artificial intelligence investment and corporate borrowing.
Investors were assessing comments from Federal Reserve Chair Kevin Warsh, who declined to indicate the likely outcome of the central bank's October interest rate meeting despite acknowledging inflationary pressures.
Elsewhere, reports suggested that investors were reconsidering the potential valuation of artificial intelligence developer Anthropic (NASDAQ:ANTP), while Taiwan was reportedly examining tighter restrictions on advanced semiconductor exports to China.
Tesla (NASDAQ:TSLA) also attracted attention after demonstrating a redesigned Roadster equipped with rocket-inspired propulsion technology.
Dow and S&P 500 Futures Advance After Wall Street Recovery
At 02:59 ET (06:59 GMT), Dow Jones futures had increased by 75 points, or 0.1%, while S&P 500 futures rose by seven points, also representing a 0.1% gain. Nasdaq 100 futures were broadly unchanged.
The modest increases followed a positive session for the main US stock indices on Thursday, when equities recovered from declines earlier in the week.
Those losses had been associated with concerns about the substantial borrowing used to finance artificial intelligence infrastructure and the effect of elevated interest rates on technology companies.
CoreWeave (NASDAQ:CRWV), whose sharp share price decline on Wednesday contributed to wider selling across AI-related stocks, stabilised during Thursday's session.
Analysts at ING said the recovery had eased some immediate concerns, although uncertainty surrounding inflation and technology valuations remained.
"Financial markets have calmed a little after CoreWeave stock bounced on Thursday. But the debate between intensifying price pressures needing tighter monetary policy and a tech correction requiring a softer policy outlook continues," the analysts said.
Federal Reserve Chair Kevin Warsh Declines to Signal October Rate Decision
Federal Reserve Chair Kevin Warsh offered a positive assessment of the US economy during an event in Thailand, noting its resilience despite tighter monetary conditions.
However, he also acknowledged concerns about rising prices following discussions with central bank officials from other countries.
Warsh said it was "hard to be in a discussion" with fellow central bankers and "not compare notes and hear the same thing about the surge in prices."
Despite these concerns, he declined to provide specific guidance on the Federal Reserve's next interest rate decision.
Warsh said he did not "believe in saying now what I might do at the meetings that we have in the balance of this year."
His comments maintained the central bank's flexibility ahead of its scheduled policy meeting on 27–28 October.
The Federal Reserve increased interest rates by 25 basis points in September as part of its efforts to bring inflation back towards its 2% target.
Although some policymakers have indicated that additional increases may be necessary, they have also emphasised the importance of assessing incoming economic data.
Market expectations currently favour unchanged rates at the October meeting.
Expectations of an immediate increase briefly strengthened following higher-than-anticipated US consumer inflation figures earlier in the week, but subsequently eased.
Investors are instead considering the possibility of another 25-basis-point increase at the Federal Reserve's final meeting of the year in December.
Anthropic Investors Reassess Potential IPO Valuation
Some existing and prospective investors in Anthropic (NASDAQ:ANTP) are reportedly reconsidering the company's potential valuation ahead of a possible initial public offering, according to Reuters.
The developer of the Claude artificial intelligence chatbot has reportedly been considering a stock market flotation as early as November.
Following investor meetings in San Francisco on Wednesday, some participants discussed a valuation of approximately $1.5 trillion, compared with an earlier estimate of around $2 trillion.
The lower figure represents a potential reduction of approximately 25% from the previously discussed valuation.
According to media reports, Anthropic told investors that its third-quarter revenue had increased by 45% to $16.8 billion.
The reported increase indicated continued demand for its artificial intelligence products, although analysts highlighted a slowdown in the rate of revenue growth compared with the preceding period.
The valuation discussions remain preliminary, and no final IPO pricing or timetable was confirmed in the report.
Taiwan Considers Tighter AI Semiconductor Export Restrictions
Taiwan is reportedly examining additional restrictions on exports of advanced artificial intelligence semiconductors to China.
Bloomberg News reported that the proposed measures would bring Taiwan's export controls into closer alignment with those of the United States.
The discussions are understood to focus on strengthening authorities' ability to prevent the diversion of advanced computing equipment, including AI servers containing Nvidia processors, from Taiwan to mainland China.
The proposed restrictions follow concerns that some advanced chips have continued to reach China despite US export controls introduced in 2022.
Reports have identified possible smuggling routes through Southeast Asia.
Additional Taiwanese measures could increase trade tensions with Beijing, which has previously criticised American restrictions on semiconductor exports.
The report did not specify when Taiwan might introduce the proposed controls or whether a final decision had been made.
Tesla Demonstrates Roadster With Rocket-Inspired Thrusters
Tesla (NASDAQ:TSLA) unveiled a redesigned version of its Roadster electric sports car on Thursday evening during an event at SpaceX's testing facility in McGregor, Texas.
The vehicle incorporated propulsion technology developed using SpaceX expertise, allowing it to perform aerial manoeuvres during the demonstration.
Tesla showed the Roadster travelling up a curved ramp before leaving the surface, rotating through a full inversion and using air thrusters to adjust its orientation before landing on all four wheels.
The company also demonstrated the vehicle jumping over parked cars and a semi-truck.
Chief Executive Elon Musk said production of the redesigned Roadster was expected to begin in 2028.
The proposed timetable comes more than a decade after Tesla first presented plans for a new generation of its electric sports car.
Tesla shares gained more than 3% in extended trading following the presentation.
The demonstration illustrated the vehicle's proposed capabilities, although commercial production remains subject to the company's development schedule.
Markets Assess Monetary Policy, AI Valuations and Technology Developments
Friday's early market movements reflected a combination of recovering technology shares, uncertainty about US interest rates and developments across the artificial intelligence industry.
US futures indicated modest gains following Thursday's recovery, while investors continued to monitor concerns about inflation and the financing of large-scale technology investment.
The Federal Reserve's October meeting remains an important event for financial markets, with expectations currently favouring unchanged interest rates.
Meanwhile, Anthropic's reported valuation discussions, potential Taiwanese semiconductor export restrictions and Tesla's Roadster demonstration provided additional developments for technology investors to assess.

Oil Prices Head for Weekly Gain as Hormuz Attacks Rattle Markets
Crude oil prices were on course to book a weekly gain earlier today, amid continuing Iranian strikes on tankers in the Strait of Hormuz and a substantial inventory draw in the United States, at 8.56 million barrels for the week to October 9.
At the time of writing, Brent crude was trading at $105.48 per barrel, with West Texas Intermediate at $97.40 per barrel. Both were off highs touched earlier in the week, but still higher than last Friday.
"Uncertainty in the Middle East and concerns over the sustainability of oil flows through the Strait of Hormuz have the market pricing in a significant risk premium," ING's commodity team said in a note earlier on Friday. "The longer this pickup in flows continues, the more likely the market will start giving back some of this risk premium," Warren Patterson and Ewa Manthey said, adding, however, that "In order to see a full removal, there needs to be some kind of agreement between the US and Iran."
Kpler, meanwhile, reported that Iranian strikes on tankers in the Strait of Hormuz had driven oil flows sharply down. In the week to October 13, the daily average dropped to 11.62 million barrels, from a baseline of 17.15 million barrels daily, Reuters reported today.
The security risks in the Persian Gulf have contributed significantly to higher oil prices, and recent reports about attempts to rein those in have failed to make an impression. Among these was the IEA's announcement of an inventory release totalling 100 million barrels, which represents the remaining deliveries from the 400-million-barrel emergency release agreed in March 2026. President Trump also suggested lifting sanctions on some so-called shadow fleet tankers in order to bring down record-high freight rates, resulting from a squeeze on tanker availability.
ING's commodity analysts questioned the availability of those tankers, since they are already in use, despite the sanctions. They also pointed to EU sanctions still in place for those vessels, making insuring them a potential problem.

### 7. ★★★☆☆ Trump denies limiting Ukraine access to Starlink network

- **分类**: 🌍 地缘政治
- **来源**: BBC World / GN Geopolitics / NPR World / Al Jazeera
- **多源确认**: 4
- **时间**: 2026-10-16T19:33:48+00:00
- **发生了什么**: Speed curbs limited Ukraine's defences against deadly Russian attacks on Tuesday night.

<details>
<summary><strong>原文 / 抓取到的正文</strong></summary>

US President Donald Trump has denied limiting Ukraine&#x27;s access to Starlink&#x27;s sister internet network Starshield after it restricted drone speed, hampering Kyiv&#x27;s ability to protect itself from Russian attacks.
"It wasn&#x27;t me and I don&#x27;t think it was [Starlink owner] Elon [Musk]," Trump said, hours after Ukrainian President Volodymyr Zelensky said speed had yet to be restored.
Musk himself suggested the US government might have been behind the restrictions, while Reuters news agency reported on Thursday that the order had come from inside the White House.
Ukraine was hit by several deadly strikes on Tuesday after the network slowdown.
Starlink is a civilian-use high-speed internet network operated by Musk&#x27;s SpaceX. Starshield - which relies on Starlink- is its military-grade sister service that was designed for the Pentagon.
The US had previously warned it could withhold sharing intelligence, crucial for Kyiv&#x27;s defences, if Ukraine did not stop hitting Russian oil refineries.
Tension has been brewing between Washington and Kyiv for days over a recent controversial diesel deal between Russia and the US.
Zelensky - angered by the deal - resumed targeting Russian oil refineries this week in a move criticised by Trump after he announced a ceasefire on energy infrastructure. Kyiv said it would only stop the strikes if Moscow did the same.
"If I don&#x27;t stand up to this... who else will?" Zelensky asked EU leaders at a summit on Friday.
Trump had also said that Zelensky was "more difficult to deal with than [Russian President Vladimir] Putin".
But speaking to reporters as he left the White House on Friday, the US president said he had a good relationship with his Ukrainian counterpart.
Asked about Starshield, he said he was "not sure" it "ever came down".
Musk earlier explained that Starshield was managed by the US government, and that the Pentagon could have been responsible.
In its report about the alleged order regarding Starshield, Reuters quoted two US officials and a Western official briefed on the matter.
The US government has allowed Ukraine to use Starlink and Starshield in its defence against Russia. Musk cut Moscow&#x27;s access to the service in February, giving Kyiv an advantage in the conflict.
On Tuesday night, Starlink restricted speed limits to 100mph (160km/h), meaning any weapons that travelled faster than that lost connectivity to the network.
Some 600,000 in Ukraine were left without power as communications and energy facilities used by Ukraine&#x27;s military were also hit in the deadly attacks.
A bipartisan group of US senators has signed a letter demanding an investigation into the disruptions to the network.
"Restrictions reportedly degraded Ukraine&#x27;s ability to conduct defensive operations against Russian drone and missile attacks on civilian infrastructure," the letter read.
On Friday, French President Emmanuel Macron vowed that EU states would not abandon Ukraine.
Speaking at the end of the two-day summit in Brussels attended by Zelensky, Macron said it was Europe&#x27;s job to hold the line in the face of some people&#x27;s "ambiguities". That was apparently a reference to signs of wavering US support for Ukraine.
Funding for Ukraine and helping it get through another freezing winter - its fifth since Russia&#x27;s full-scale invasion in 2022 - topped the summit&#x27;s agenda.
The war will cost â¬155bn (Â£131bn; $174bn) next year, Zelensky told the European Council, with at least â¬70bn needed to come from the EU for defence and weapons.
Macron said Europe was working to help Ukraine with its satellite and early warning systems, and Ukraine&#x27;s friends would look at its request for further aid.
Zelensky says Ukraine has right to defend itself after Trump calls latest strikes a &#x27;disgrace&#x27;

### 8. ★★★☆☆ Ships in Strait of Hormuz still face "indiscriminate attacks," maritime monitor warns - CBS News

<details>
<summary><strong>原文 / 抓取到的正文</strong></summary>

Follow updates on the war in the Middle East for Friday, Oct. 16, here . See earlier developments below.
Ships remain exposed to "indiscriminate attacks" in the Strait of Hormuz, even if they try to hide their movements by switching off tracking systems, a maritime monitor said Thursday, confirming three strikes on vessels in the waterway this week.
The Houthis on Thursday launched a strike against the presidential palace in government-controlled Yemen. The attack caused multiple death and injuries among the security staff, according to the internationally recognized government .
The Saudi-led coalition battling the Iran-backed Houthis in Yemen said Thursday it had intercepted a cruise missile over Saudi Arabia's western Medina region. The Houthis called the claim " a shameless and brazen lie ."
Iran president says conflict in Mideast amounts to "ecocide"
Iranian President Masoud Pezeshkian on Friday urged countries to condemn what he called "ecocide" in the Middle East caused by "wars of aggression" against his country.
Pezeshkian said the conflicts in Gaza and the wider Middle East had taken a significant toll on nature that extended beyond national borders and required "international solutions."
"What has occurred in our region as a result of wars of aggression is ecocide, or environmental genocide, and it must be condemned," Pezeshkian said at a nature summit in Armenia that was attended by United Nations Secretary-General Antonio Guterres.
Pezeshkian decried an "atmosphere of distrust" between Iran and the U.S. in a recent interview with CBS News .
"As long as they don't cease doing what they're imposing on us every day, it's difficult to trust them," he said. "Every day, they increase the sanctions against us. Every day, they threaten us. Every day, they strike us, and they don't honor what they have put in writing. So, if they engage in talks, will they actually follow through?"
https://www.cbsnews.com/live-updates/iran-war-us-houthi-saudi-arabia-trump/#post-update-ee7946dd
Rubio tells Lebanon's president not to return seized explosives to Hezbollah
Secretary of State Marco Rubio urged Lebanon's president on Thursday not to return several hundred kilograms of explosives which Lebanese authorities seized last week from the Iran-backed militant group Hezbollah, a State Department official told CBS News.
In a call, Rubio asked Lebanese President Joseph Aoun "to hold accountable all those who ordered, financed, and directed the shipment, and underscored that returning explosives to Hezbollah should be out of the question," the official said.
The explosives had been seized on a road linking eastern Lebanon to Beirut, a Hezbollah lawmaker acknowledged earlier Thursday.
Rubio on the call also brought up "Lebanon's 1955 Boycott Law," the official said, which prohibits any contact between Lebanese and Israeli citizens.
Rubio told Aoun the law is "routinely used for politicized prosecutions that deter steps towards peace," the official said. The secretary of state called the law "impractical and urged direct military-to-military communication between Lebanese and Israeli forces for deconfliction," the U.S. official added.
This comes a day after the White House criticized a legal complaint that was filed in Lebanon against the country's ambassador to Washington over her reported outreach to her Israeli counterpart.
https://www.cbsnews.com/live-updates/iran-war-us-houthi-saudi-arabia-trump/#post-update-38eb1eba
White House prepares plans to use weapon dubbed "mother of all bombs" on Iran, sources say
The White House and Pentagon have prepared plans to use a Massive Ordnance Air Blast bomb to hit military sites in Iran if ordered by President Trump, sources familiar with the matter told CBS News.
A Pentagon official told CBS News: "The Department of War plans for a wide range of operational scenarios to execute Presidential and Secretary level direction and to ensure mission success in any operational environment."
A White House spokesperson said: "We do not comment on sensitive military plans that may or may not exist. President Trump keeps all options on the table to ensure that Iran can never have a nuclear weapon."
Last week, Mr. Trump said the U.S. would not attack Iran before the midterm elections. He also touted "productive conversations" with Iran, though he did not provide specifics.
CNN first reported Thursday on the plans to use the Massive Ordnance Air Blast Bomb, or MOAB, on Iran.
Mr. Trump set a precedent for using the MOAB, also known as the "mother of all bombs," when it was used in 2017 to attack an Islamic State cave complex in Afghanistan. The bomb is the largest U.S. non-nuclear weapon, weighing upwards of 30,000 pounds at a cost of $170,000 per unit.
https://www.cbsnews.com/live-updates/iran-war-us-houthi-saudi-arabia-trump/#post-update-dcb18e23
Ousted general warned of years-long missile defense shortage before Iran war
In late December, two months before the U.S. war with Iran began, Army Chief of Staff Gen. Randy George warned that his service was critically short on missile defense munitions and that the shortage would last another five years, according to multiple U.S. officials familiar with the matter. Defense Secretary Pete Hegseth ousted George in April, as the Iran war was underway.
George's risk analysis appeared in the Pentagon's Global Force Management Allocation Plan, a defense secretary-approved annual order determining where troops and weapons will be allocated across the globe, according to the U.S. officials who spoke to CBS News.
He warned that the stockpile of Terminal High Altitude Area Defense batteries and Patriot missiles would continue to be strategically low until 2030. His assessment was written for the Defense Department's plan for fiscal year 2027 and before the outbreak of the U.S.-Israel-led war with Iran, now in its seventh month.
U.S. officials who spoke to CBS News under condition of anonymity because they were not authorized to speak publicly said his assessment stemmed from the transfer of Patriots to Ukraine after Russia's invasion in 2022, the expenditure of munitions to defend Israel, and the reduced capacity of the defense industrial base.

The Saudi International, the final event of the Asian Tour golf season, was postponed on Thursday following several deadly attacks on the kingdom by Yemen's Iran-backed Houthis.
The tournament was originally scheduled to take place from Nov. 18 to 21 in Riyadh and no alternative date has been announced.
"The Asian Tour remains in direct contact with its players and members regarding the schedule and next steps," read a joint statement from Saudi Golf and the Asian Tour, saying the competition was postponed due to "logistical challenges."
The tournament, which was part of the European Tour from 2019 to 2021, is backed by Saudi Arabia's Private Investment Fund.
The postponement follows that of the Six Kings Slam tennis exhibition, which was pushed back from next week until December on Wednesday.
Earlier this week, three Saudi Pro League football matches, including one involving Cristiano Ronaldo's Al Nassr, were also postponed.

Lebanese president asks Rubio to press Israel to stop "assaults" in southern Lebanon
Lebanese President Joseph Aoun asked U.S. Secretary of State Marco Rubio to press Israel on its assaults on southern Lebanon and stand by the trilateral agreement signed in June .
The president's office said on X it received a call from Rubio "during which they discussed the general situation in Lebanon and the region in light of recent developments."
Aoun said he requested Israel stop bulldozing operations, enable farmers to use their fields and stop targeting them, according to the Lebanese president's office.
Israel patrols a so-called "security zone" in southern Lebanon to prevent attacks by the Iran-backed Hezbollah militant group. As part of the trilateral agreement, Israel was supposed to progressively give control over to official Lebanese army forces. However, Israel has said they will only do so when Hezbollah completely disarms, which the group said it will not do.
Aoun told Rubio that Lebanese forces have been "fully carrying out their duties in implementing decisions related to preventing armed displays" in a nod to them carrying out their part of the trilateral agreement.
The State Department has not yet released a readout of the call from its side.

The Houthis carried out a missile attack on the Yemeni government's presidential palace in the interim capital of Aden on Thursday, with government military sources reporting four killed.
Residents of the southern city reported hearing explosions on Thursday, while a journalist saw smoke rising from the site, which also serves as the headquarters of the internationally recognized government.

Four people were killed and 10 others wounded in the attack, government military sources later said.

Kallas described the Houthi attacks as "indiscriminate" and "totally unacceptable," stressing that the EU was acting as a security provider in the region through its Operation ASPIDES naval mission.

Trump administration considering easing Iran sanctions to reduce shipping costs, Reuters says
The United States is considering easing sanctions on Iran's shadow fleet tankers to reduce record-high global shipping costs, the Reuters news agency reported Thursday, citing an unnamed U.S. official.
The proposal would allow companies, subject to U.S. Treasury approval, to transport non-sanctioned oil on previously sanctioned vessels, according to the Reuters report.
However, the move would be unlikely to significantly lower freight rates, as many tankers are old and insurers would remain bound by EU and U.K. restrictions, market analysis firm Energy Aspects told its clients, Reuters reported.

A German court on Thursday jailed two men convicted of planning attacks on prominent pro-Israel public figures and spying on Jews on behalf of Iranian secret services.
The court in Hamburg sentenced a Danish national named as Ali S. to eight-and-a-half years in prison for planning deadly attacks on targets including the head of the German-Israeli society, Volker Beck, and the head of the Central Council of Jews in Germany, Josef Schuster.
An Afghan accomplice, Tawab M., was sentenced to four-and-a-half years in prison. Prosecutors said he had agreed to recruit a third man and provide him with a weapon for the attack on Beck.
S. took orders from the Quds Force, the elite foreign operations branch of Iran's Islamic Revolutionary Guard Corps, in early 2025, prosecutors said.
The two men were remanded in custody in Germany following their arrests and extraditions last year in Denmark.
Their convictions came days after Britain's domestic spy chief warned of Iran's evolving "hybrid tactics" aimed largely at Jewish communities and groups in the U.K. and across Europe.

Yemen urges U.N. to take urgent action to lift Houthi siege of Taiz
Yemen's government has urged the United Nations and the U.N. Security Council to take urgent action to end what it described as a tightening Houthi siege of the southwest city of Taiz, warning of a looming humanitarian catastrophe affecting more than four million people.
In a letter sent on Wednesday to senior U.N. officials and the International Committee of the Red Cross, Foreign Minister Afrah Al-Zouba warned that the Houthis' escalating siege on the government-held city had worsened humanitarian conditions, including in areas around Taiz where displaced people have sought shelter.
The government called in its statement for the immediate lifting of the Houthi siege, the reopening of all access routes for civilians and supply chains, and unimpeded access to food, fuel and water. It also urged stronger monitoring and accountability mechanisms to document alleged violations and hold those responsible to account.
Al-Zouba called for concrete international measures to protect civilians and prevent access to humanitarian essentials being used for political pressure.
She said the situation required urgent action beyond expressions of concern, including ending the siege, restoring essential supplies and protecting civilian facilities.

Saudi Arabia says cruise missile intercepted in the kingdom's Medina region
The Saudi-led coalition battling the Iran-backed Houthis in Yemen said Thursday that it had intercepted a cruise missile over the Medina region in western Saudi Arabia.
"Interception and destruction of a cruise missile in the Medina region," coalition spokesman Turki al-Maliki said in a post on X .
He did not say where the missile came from, but the Houthis, who have been peppering the kingdom with airstrikes over the past week, said "the Saudi enemy's claims of an attack on Medina are a shameless and brazen lie ."
Medina is the second holiest city in Islam, after Mecca. In September, the Saudis accused the Houthis of attacking Mecca, the holiest city for Muslims, which they denied.

"Strait of Hormuz is closed," says Iranian naval officer, citing U.S. gas prices
An Iranian naval officer renewed Iran's claim on Thursday that the Strait of Hormuz is closed, saying U.S. gas prices wouldn't be so high otherwise.
"The Strait of Hormuz is closed; if it were not closed, oil would be passing through it and gas prices in the United States would not have increased," Iranian Army Coordination Deputy Rear Admiral Habibollah Sayyari told reporters on the sidelines of a ceremony honouring army nurses in Tehran, according to Iran's official IRNA news agency .
He said the naval forces of Iran's Islamic Revolutionary Guard Corps' had a strong presence west of the strait, while the Iranian army was deployed east of the waterway and as far north as the Gulf of Oman, exercising what he called "100% control and supervision" over the area.
"If any transit takes place, it must be coordinated; otherwise, you can see in the news what happens," Sayyari said.

Ships in Strait of Hormuz remain exposed to "indiscriminate attacks," says monitor, as 3 more strikes confirmed
Ships in the Strait of Hormuz remain exposed to "indiscriminate attacks," regardless of the nations to which they are registered, the cargo they are carrying, their direction of travel, or whether they turn off their tracking devices, Greek maritime monitor Marisks said Thursday.
Marisks said it had confirmed that three merchant vessels were struck by unidentified projectiles on Tuesday - incidents it said "demonstrate the indiscriminate nature of the current threat environment in the Strait of Hormuz."
Marisks said the vessels were all navigating with their tracking devices turned off, a common tactic for boats trying to get through the waterway unnoticed, which shows it "does not provide effective protection against attack," the monitor said.
The continued strikes on vessels in the strait show that, "although successful passages continue," ships in the strait are exposing themselves to a "critical and highly unpredictable maritime security threat," Marisks wrote, "with merchant vessels exposed to potentially indiscriminate attacks regardless of flag, cargo or direction of transit."
Three of the ships struck this week were operated by the same firm, they added in their bulletin, which also "raises concerns regarding possible operator-linked targeting."

Iran must be prepared for war even as it negotiates with the U.S., the country's parliament speaker said Wednesday.
Mohammad Bagher Qalibaf said in a televised speech that his country's main concern is preventing the U.S. from attacking again. But, he added, a signed agreement with Washington is no guarantee.

Trump on reports U.S. considering Iran strikes before midterms: "Leakers should be shot"
Speaking about news reports that the U.S. is considering potential strikes against Iran before the midterms in November, President Trump said Wednesday it was his opinion that "leakers should be shot."
"In the old days, we used to shoot leakers," Mr. Trump said during a campaign rally speech in Bullhead City, Arizona. "When they leak what time you're going to attack a country, and the reason you're attacking is really for defense, because you don't want them to have a weapon that can blow up millions of people in one shot."
"Now, leakers should be dealt with very, very strongly," he added. "To me, they're the scum of the earth."
The Atlantic first reported on Oct. 7 that the Pentagon was developing strike options against Iranian targets, but no final decisions had been made. The next day, the president said on Truth Social that the U.S. would not attack Iran before the midterm elections, which take place on Nov. 3.

Yemen wants training, intelligence assistance from U.S., foreign minister says
Yemen has asked the U.S. for training and intelligence support for the Yemeni armed forces and coast guard, as well as continued political, economic and humanitarian support, but their foreign minister made no mention of military support in a briefing to reporters Wednesday.
Afrah Alzouba, Yemen's first female foreign minister, spoke in a briefing on several topics related to the ongoing Yemen-Houthi war, but did not explicitly mention requests for military support. In addition to intelligence and training, Alzouba requested the U.S. expand sanctions against the Houthis. She also asked for the EU and Washington to coordinate more closely on sanctions.
She struck an aggressive tone when asked about possible negotiations with the Houthi militia, saying, "We are not calling for starting any negotiations." The Yemeni government eventually wants a peaceful settlement but is prioritizing military operations to end Houthi control, she said.
She also emphasized Yemeni ownership of ongoing operations with air support from coalition partners: "The decision is Yemeni. The command is Yemeni. The soldiers are Yemenis."
The Saudis are the strongest backers of the official Yemeni government forces, which has earned the ire of the Houthis. They have repeatedly targeted civilian infrastructure in Saudi Arabia in recent weeks, including a strike that killed 13 people at Riyadh's King Khalid International Airport. A four-year ceasefire between the two sides fell apart this summer.
Alzouba said the contacts cited last month by President Trump between the U.S. and Houthis, which surprised some in the U.S., involved "warnings" against further attacks. She did not elaborate on timing or channels.

A look at the Iran- and Russia-linked hybrid attacks the U.K. spy chief says are flourishing
The head of Britain's top domestic spy agency MI5, Sir Ken McCallum, painted a dark picture Tuesday of the changing national security threats facing the U.K. and the world.
"We have entered a new era of national security, requiring a new response," he said in his annual threat update at MI5 headquarters in London. "Those who mean the U.K. harm aren't attacking things government owns or controls. &hellip; They're aiming at people, businesses, communities."
Iran has used and continues "using criminal proxies to try to intimidate oppositionists, Iranian media organizations, and Israeli sites," McCallum said.
But analysts have also seen "Iran, like Russia, attempting to expand their hybrid campaign," he said, leading to more than 50 arrests in the U.K. over the last year alone.
"Sadly, much of this aggression has been aimed at British Jewish communities," McCallum said, speaking of incidents this year that included "a wave of arson and sabotage incidents targeting Jewish communities in the U.K. and other European nations," but also acts targeting "harder targets, like military sites."

Attacks in Strait of Hormuz reach highest level since start of war, data shows
At least 10 vessels have been attacked in or near the Strait of Hormuz since Sunday, a high since war broke out between the United States, Israel and Iran, security data showed Wednesday.
Four vessels were struck on Tuesday and six on Sunday &mdash; the highest number of attacks in a single day since the start of the conflict &mdash; according to data from security firm Vanguard, the International Maritime Organization and the British maritime agency UKMTO.
Two seafarers have been reported missing and a container ship reportedly sank as a result of recent attacks, according to the maritime sources.
Merchant vessels transiting the vital passage were first targeted by Iran in March in retaliation for U.S.-Israeli strikes on its territory, and Tehran has continued to strike vessels passing through the strait via routes it has not approved.
The attacks have disrupted shipping through the strait, which is normally one of the busiest routes for exporting oil and other commodities from Gulf countries.
Twenty-three attacks have been recorded since the start of October, almost double the average monthly number of incidents since the start of the war.
"Gulf oil flows have been recovering, with traffic through Hormuz recently returning to around 80 percent of pre-war levels despite the conflict," Vanguard analyst Barun Gupta told AFP on Wednesday.
"Increasing the tempo of attacks therefore gives Tehran a way of demonstrating that it can still disrupt that recovery and impose costs on commercial shipping and global energy markets," Gupta continued.

Yemeni government speaks to Chinese officials about Houthis developing advanced missiles
The Yemeni government has spoken with Beijing about the Houthis' alleged attempts to utilize Chinese-made technology for weapons, a Yemeni minister said on Wednesday.
"The Chinese are partners and we have discussed with them about, you know, the use of dual products," Foreign Minister Afrah al-Zouba said in response to an AFP question about reports the Iran-backed group was trying to secure Chinese components to help manufacture weapons.
"They were very keen for any sort of information so they can investigate it. Their support to the Yemeni government is clear," Zouba added.
The minister spoke to journalists via a virtual press conference to address the war in Yemen, which has also drawn in the government's main backer, Saudi Arabia.

Saudi-Houthi war in Yemen cutting off food assistance to 800,000 people, U.N. says
The escalating war between the Iran-backed Houthis and Saudi-backed Yemeni government forces is cutting off lifesaving food supplies to about 800,000 people in the region, according to the U.N. World Food Programme .
Many parts of Taiz, Yemen's third-largest city, which is located in Houthi-controlled territory, is completely inaccessible to the nonprofit, the WFP said. There are more than 600,000 people, including 200,000 women and children, in Taiz who depend on humanitarian aid, according to the agency.
"We are critically concerned about the escalating conflict, but even more so about the growing number of people we can no longer reach because of it," El-Khidir Daloum, WFP representative and country director in Yemen, said in a statement Wednesday. "Many of the families affected have been displaced multiple times and were already struggling to afford even basic food items. This is the kind of situation that can rapidly spiral into a humanitarian catastrophe if assistance cannot move safely and reach those who need it most."
The hunger situation in Yemen is among the worst in the world. Ten out of 16 districts in Yemen are categorized as being at emergency levels of food insecurity, the WFP said. About 500,000 children in Yemen under the age of 5 suffer from severe acute malnutrition, according to the group.
There are also 37 WFP employees currently being held by the Houthis, the group said, calling for their immediate release.

Saudi Arabia postpones Netflix tennis tournament featuring top stars
The chairman of Saudi Arabia's General Entertainment Authority on Wednesday said the exhibition Six Kings Slam tennis tournament has been postponed for logistical reasons, after Yemen's Houthis carried out several deadly attacks on the kingdom.
"The Six Kings Slam tournament has been postponed due to logistical reasons and will now take place on December 27," wrote Turki Alalshikh on X, sharing a statement from the Riyadh Season entertainment program.
The tournament, while not officially sanctioned as an ATP event, features some of the top players in the world vying for millions of dollars and airs live on Netflix. It had initially been scheduled to air Oct. 21 to 24.
The men's-only event features Carlos Alcaraz, Novak Djokovic, Alexander Zverev, Taylor Fritz, Alex De Minaur and Learner Tien, who replaced Jannik Sinner due to injury.
The event premiered in 2024 with Sinner winning each of the first two competitions. Sinner, Alcaraz, Djokovic or Zverev has won every major since Wimbledon in 2022.

Israel signs deal with Pentagon to acquire 12 Apache attack helicopters
Israel has signed a deal with the Pentagon to acquire 12 Apache helicopters from Boeing, according to the Israeli Ministry of Defense.
"The procurement of Apache helicopters is a critical step in building the IDF's force capabilities; it will strengthen operational readiness against a wide range of threats and contribute to the security of the State of Israel for years to come," Israeli Defense Minister Israel Katz said in a statement.
The helicopters, the newest Apache E model, can carry 16 HELLFIRE missiles, 76 2.75-inch rockets and 1,200 30 mm chain gun rounds, according to Boeing.

"What happened was that because the Houthis started attacking the Saudi pipeline and other Iranian proxies from Iraq were attacking refineries and so on, we had a negative shock to energy again in September," Hassett told Fox Business on Wednesday.

Saudi offers alternative travel routes for investment conference: AFP sources
Saudi Arabia plans to move ahead with a major investment conference in Riyadh despite Houthi attacks on the kingdom, offering on-site and virtual attendance as well as alternative air and land travel routes, diplomatic sources told AFP.
Riyadh usually hosts a packed calendar of events, but it has recently seen escalating military tensions with the Iran-backed Yemeni group, which has intensified attacks on the kingdom's airports and oil facilities.
Saudi Arabia's Future Investment Initiative Institute hosts an annual forum dubbed "Davos in the desert," which usually draws foreign leaders and business titans, and this year it is scheduled to be held in Riyadh from Oct. 26 to 29.
In order to prevent cancellation, organizers reached out to embassies and foreign companies to explore "hybrid" participation options, allowing officials and business executives to attend either in person or virtually, diplomatic sources said.
They have even discussed arrangements for participants to fly by private jet to Dhahran airport in eastern Saudi Arabia before traveling by road to Riyadh.
A diplomat from an Asian country said "they contacted us and offered that attendees travel to Manama or Doha," referring to the capitals of Bahrain and Qatar respectively, and then drive by land to the Saudi capital.
Riyadh's international airport, a main target for Houthi attacks, suspended operations last week after 13 were killed in a strike there, with no clear timeline for when air traffic would reopen.

U.S. inflation accelerated to 3.7% as energy costs soared
Inflation accelerated across the U.S. in September, rising at an annual rate of 3.7% as energy prices soared, according to new government data.
Inflation picked up after two months of holding steady at an annual pace of 3.4%. Core CPI, which excludes the more volatile energy and gas categories, rose 2.6% from a year ago, accelerating from a 2.4% rate in August and higher than economists' forecast of 2.5%.
Inflation has risen sharply since the start of the year due to soaring energy prices, sparked by the war in Iran and Russia's war with Ukraine.
The Labor Department said Wednesday that gasoline prices, which rose 9.1% in September from the prior month, accounted for more than half the monthly increase in the CPI report. Energy prices rose 20.4% from a year ago, while gasoline jumped 34.2%.
Prices at the pump have cooled slightly in recent weeks but remain elevated. The national average for a gallon of gasoline was $4.33 a gallon on Wednesday, AAA reported, up from $2.98 before the war started. The national average for diesel, which hit a record high in late September, was $6.29 a gallon.

Lebanese, Iranian foreign leaders discuss situation in the Middle East
Lebanese President Joseph Aoun received a call from Iranian Foreign Minister Abbas Araghchi on Wednesday to discuss the situation in the Middle East, according to the Lebanese president's office.
Both sides "discussed the current situation in the region in light of recent developments and agreed on the necessity of supporting diplomatic solutions as the best means to put an end to the conflicts in the region," the Lebanese presidency said in a statement about the call.
The Iran-backed Hezbollah militant group has been waging war with Israel in the southern part of Lebanon for months, with the official Lebanese government attempting to cool the conflict down with ceasefire agreements. Those have all been dismissed and ignored by Hezbollah.
Meanwhile, Iranian Deputy Foreign Minister Saeed Khatibzadeh arrived in Lebanon for talks with several groups. He was greeted by Lebanese Parliament Speaker Nabih Berri, who is pro-Hezbollah, and other Hezbollah representatives, according to the official Iranian news outlet IRNA.
The meeting comes amid an increase in attacks by another Iran-backed proxy group, the Yemen-based Houthis.
"During his visit, Khatibzadeh is scheduled to hold a series of meetings with senior Lebanese officials and prominent figures to discuss bilateral relations and explore opportunities to broaden cooperation between the two countries," according to IRNA.

Iran says cargo ship sinks after hitting naval mine in Strait of Hormuz
Iran's Islamic Revolutionary Guard Corps said Wednesday that a cargo ship had sunk after hitting a naval mine in the Strait of Hormuz. It is the first time Iran has reported a ship sinking after hitting a mine during the war.
There were few details, including about the fate of the crew. Iranian state TV quoted a statement from the IRGC saying the ship was traveling on what Iran considers an illegal and dangerous route - likely the shipping lanes the U.S. has urged vessels to use, close to Oman's coast - when it hit the mine Wednesday morning.
It said the ship was carrying 2,500 containers when it first caught fire. Iran has repeatedly warned ships against trying to transit the strait without its approval.
At the end of August, President Trump declared the strait free of sea mines , saying U.S. forces had "totally cleared the strait of mines &hellip; Any boat goes through with a mine, we take it out."
There was no immediate verification of the sinking and no report by the United Kingdom Maritime Trade Operations monitor.

### 11. ★★★☆☆ Three years on, Israel still bars foreign journalists from entering Gaza

- **分类**: 🌍 地缘政治
- **来源**: NPR World
- **多源确认**: 1
- **时间**: 2026-10-15T21:01:50+00:00
- **发生了什么**: More than a year into a declared ceasefire in Gaza — and three years into the war — Israel still hasn't allowed foreign media independent access to Gaza.

<details>
<summary><strong>原文 / 抓取到的正文</strong></summary>

Three years on, Israel still bars foreign journalists from entering Gaza More than a year into a declared ceasefire in Gaza — and three years into the war — Israel still hasn't allowed foreign media independent access to Gaza.
Three years on, Israel still bars foreign journalists from entering Gaza
Three years on, Israel still bars foreign journalists from entering Gaza
< iframe src="https://www.npr.org/player/embed/nx-s1-6000378/nx-s1-9966711" width="100%" height="290" frameborder="0" scrolling="no" title="NPR embedded audio player">
More than a year into a declared ceasefire in Gaza — and three years into the war — Israel still hasn't allowed foreign media independent access to Gaza.
: [POST-BROADCAST CORRECTION Oct. 16, 2026: An earlier version of this story incorrectly said that 247 Palestinian journalists have been killed by Israel's military in Gaza. In fact, that’s the number of journalists and people who support their work who have been killed across the Middle East since Oct. 7, 2023, according to the Committee to Protect Journalists. Of those, 189 were killed in Gaza.] SCOTT DETROW, HOST: It's been more than a year since a ceasefire was declared in the - in Gaza between Israel and Hamas, but Israel has still not allowed foreign journalists to have independent access to the enclave in more than three years. So Palestinian journalists still bear the responsibility of reporting inside Gaza while their colleagues outside lobby to come in. NPR's Kat Lonsdorf reports from Tel Aviv. KAT LONSDORF, BYLINE: The other day, a message popped up on my phone from my friend and colleague, NPR's Anas Baba in Gaza City. (SOUNDBITE OF NOTIFICATION CHIME) LONSDORF: It was a picture of the beautiful sunset he was seeing in that moment. I looked up from where I was sitting in Jaffa in southern Tel Aviv and saw the same sky, with streaks of bright pink and orange. I took a picture and texted back. (SOUNDBITE OF ELECTRONIC CHIRP) LONSDORF: Oh, wow, he wrote. That's so sweet - we're watching the same sunset, the closest ever. Gaza City and Jaffa are only about 40 miles from each other. For three years now, Anas and I have texted and talked over the phone a lot, sharing reporting and life updates, but we've never met in person. We can't. Israel bars most Palestinians from leaving Gaza, and Israel has not allowed foreign journalists to enter Gaza independently since the war started - even now, with a declared ceasefire in place for more than a year. JOE FEDERMAN: We haven't really been able to get a straight answer as to why this ban continues. LONSDORF: Joe Federman is deputy director of the Middle East for The Associated Press based in Jerusalem. He's also a longtime member of the Foreign Press Association in Israel. The FPA, of which NPR is also a member, filed a petition with Israel's Supreme Court early in the war, arguing for press access to Gaza. The court rejected that petition. The FPA filed another one. A decision on that has been postponed numerous times. FEDERMAN: I don't think anybody could have expected things to drag on for this long. LONSDORF: Israel has repeatedly said it's too dangerous for journalists to independently enter Gaza. It's allowed tightly controlled media embeds with the Israeli military into the enclave, but it controls what journalists see and where they go and does not allow any interviews with Palestinians. Now with the ceasefire in place, it is still dangerous. Israeli strikes have killed Palestinians almost daily, according to health officials there. But the FPA has argued that the level of combat is now much, much lower. FEDERMAN: This is a risk that most journalists are willing to take. We believe it's a decision the news organizations should take. LONSDORF: NPR reached out to the Israeli government to ask why press access is still limited. We did not get a response. JODIE GINSBERG: My fear is, the longer this goes on, the more normalized that lack of access becomes. LONSDORF: Jodie Ginsberg is chief executive of the Committee to Protect Journalists, an organization that promotes press freedom worldwide and has also been pressing Israel on the issue. She stresses that Palestinian journalists have been doing heroic work for years documenting the war, but Israel has repeatedly attacked their credibility. GINSBERG: And so having international journalists in who are able to corroborate what is being documented by their colleagues is also really important. LONSDORF: Israel's military has also killed many of the Palestinian journalists in Gaza - 189, according to the CPJ - saying, often without providing evidence, that they are members of Hamas. Clayton Weimers is the North American director for Reporters Without Borders, which has also been pushing for press access. CLAYTON WEIMERS: You have to ask yourself, if the policy is don't let international journalists observe what's going on, what exactly are they trying to hide? LONSDORF: That policy could change with a change in government, Weimers points out, and Israeli national elections are coming up later this month. WEIMERS: I remain optimistic, cautiously so. LONSDORF: I recently asked our colleague Anas Baba what it would mean to have foreign journalists working alongside him again. ANAS BABA, BYLINE: It would mean we are no longer carrying this burden alone. LONSDORF: He said he's trying to keep up hope. And maybe someday we'll be able to work together in person and watch the sunset from Gaza sitting side by side. Kat Lonsdorf, NPR News, Tel Aviv.

An earlier version of this story incorrectly said that 247 Palestinian journalists have been killed by Israel's military in Gaza. In fact, that’s the number of journalists and people who support their work who have been killed across the Middle East since Oct. 7, 2023, according to the Committee to Protect Journalists. Of those, 189 were killed in Gaza.

And, on that note, we will be closing the blog for today .
Ukraine’s Volodymyr Zelenskyy has asked the leaders for another €70bn next year to fund Ukraine’s defence against Russia as he joined a key EU summit in Brussels ( 10:10 , 13:44 , 15:13 , 15:26 , 16:00 ).
EU’s Ursula von der Leyen and António Costa confirmed the bloc will look at the request ( 19:01 , 19:14 , 19:20 ), as they said the EU’s support to Ukraine “matters more than ever” given growing pressures from Russia.
During a press conference in Brussels, Zelenskyy responded to US president Donald Trump’s criticism of his actions ( 13:51 , 13:56 , 14:04 ), and confirmed that Starlink satellite systems were back up, but with speeds not completely restored to previous levels ( 13:42 ).
Starlink access still not fully restored for Ukraine’s military, says Zelenskyy
Zelenskyy smiled but offered no response to a suggestion he was being used as a “scapegoat” for Donald Trump’s domestic problems ahead of next month’s midterm elections ( 14:06 ).
Zelenskyy smiles when asked if he feels he is being made a scapegoat for pressures facing Trump
German chancellor Friedrich Merz said there was a “broad consensus” on the EU’s need to keep supporting Ukraine throughout this coming winter, acknowledging Kyiv’s request for further financial assistance ( 16:45 ), and appearing to back retaliatory strikes against Russian communication and energy supply systems ( 16:49 , 16:54 ).
Several other EU leaders offered their backing for Ukraine, ( 10:10 , 10:21 , 10:25 , 10:36 , 11:09 , 11:23 ), with top EU figures saying the bloc would not lift any sanctions on Russia unless it stops the war ( 10:10 ), and criticising Trump’s diesel deal with Russia ( 10:10 , 19:32 ).
Zelenskyy declined to say if Ukraine will be targeting Russian refineries ( 13:47 ).
Power plant attacks reflect Russian blueprint to make Ukrainian cities unliveable
EU’s Costa insisted the new EU budget needed to be agreed by the end of the year, despite calls to delay its adoption considering a number of big elections in the EU ( 19:27 ).
The European Commission president, Ursula von der Leyen, insisted the bloc needed a bigger budget with “flexibilities” to respond to various crises ( 19:12 ).
Germany’s Merz conceded there were still “significant differences” between EU capitals on the budget, with talks expected to continue until the end of the year ( 16:43 , 16:54 , 16:59 ).
Pope Leo spoke with the Russian president, Vladimir Putin, this afternoon, expressing his grave concern for the humanitarian situation in the Russian-occupied Ukrainian city of Oleshky ( 17:36 ).
The Kremlin said Putin expressed his support for Leo’s ideas, but blamed Ukraine for the situation in the city ( 18:22 ).

Speaking at the same time, France’s Emmanuel Macron told reporters that he did not consider a diesel deal struck by Trump with Russia as useful.
“ This decision is not good for ‌anyone. It sends a ​negative signal. It a an element ⁠of division and ​doubt,” Macron ​said after ​an EU ​summit ‌in ​Brussels, Reuters reported.
After some concluding comments on the bloc’s response to the growing sales of Chinese electric cars in Europe , von der Leyen and Costa wrap up their press conference.
Elsewhere, US president Donald Trump insisted that neither him nor Elon Musk was responsible for Ukraine losing Starlink access.
Starlink access still not fully restored for Ukraine’s military, says Zelenskyy
He claimed the restrictions was not imposed by the US, and that he has a good relationship with Ukraine’s Zelenskyy, Reuters reported.
Costa gets asked if he is more confident that a deal can be found by the end of the year.
And there is also a question about Marine Le Pen’s suggestions that the budget should not be decided this early given so many key elections in Europe next year that are likely to reshape the political landscape of the continent.
Costa repeats Merz’s point from earlier that it usually takes about a year to implement a budget ( 16:59 ), and anything that would threaten to delay its entry into force from January 2028 puts it a risk altogether.
“This has nothing [to do] with elections,” he says, and adds if that was a consideration, there would never be a good moment as there are always elections somewhere.
“But unfortunately, the life is not only elections. We are talking about our farmers, our companies, our students, our researchers, our universities – and they are waiting for new European funds.”
Asked about Ukraine’s new financial needs, von der Leyen says that 2026 is covered, but confirms the gap for 2027 is around €70bn euros.
On migration, von der Leyen says all leaders recognise “enormous progress” made on this issue in recent years, but more needs to be done.
He says the number of illegal crossings has gone down by over 56% in the last two years, and down by 34% in the first nine months of this year.
“These trends need to continue to remain successful.”
She says there is a “new phenomenon” of state and non-state actors trying to “engineer sudden mass movements of people … to overwhelm the authorities.”
It will come as news to the likes of Poland and Lithuania that it’s anything new given the pressure from Belarus for years.
She says the EU’s support “matters more than ever.”
She says the bloc “is doing a long, but the reality is simple: as long as this war continues, Ukraine’s financing needs will keep growing.”
“Right now, the European Union and its member states account for 80% of external aid to Ukraine, and we will continue to lead. But we urgently need also other partners to step in.”
“As throughout these five years of war, no one wants more peace than the people of Ukraine, and it is our duty to support them , to be in the position to find a just and sustainable peace for Ukraine.”
Von der Leyen also mounts a defence of the commission’s proposal on the next EU budget, effectively saying that as leaders keep coming to the EU asking it to fix things, it’s understandable that it’ll need more resources.
“If there’s one lesson we have painfully learned over the last seven years, that is the lesson that we are in a very fast changing and volatile world and that there will be more crises. We just don’t know what is going to be happening. … I could only act in these crisis because of the flexibilities in the budget … and in each crisis, everybody was calling for Europe to step in.”
He says the new proposals have “even less flexibilities,” and so the commission is not happy with them.
“The question before us is not which priorities we should abandon, but how to finance these priorities.”
She points out to high energy price increases since the start of Trump’s war against Iran.
“This is the reality we have to deal with today, and we discussed this intensively with the member states. Because of the fact that the member states have very different energy mixes, we know there is no silver bullet as a solution.”
She says the G7-agreed release of oil and diesel, the EU’s extension of temporary state aid framework, and some other technical changes should provide some relief.
“For the mid term, it is important that as long as Europe depends on imported oil and gas and our prosperity remains exposed to the events beyond our control, it is important for us to stay the course of the transition; that is a transition towards affordable, clean and homegrown energy sources, … nuclear and renewables.”
She talks about the need to “modernise and improve our grid network” in Europe .
She also briefly discusses proposed changes to the ETS system and the EU’s trade with China, saying there was a “remarkable unity” on the latter point to reduce the “unsustainable” bilateral trade deficit.
Finally, on migration, Costa says the system is “working but … must still be improved.”
“Migration remains a very political issue where there are different political visions which are known to all of you. The European Council will continue to work on this issue,” he says.
On the next EU budget, Costa says the negotiations will need to conclude by the end of the year, and there was some progress made.
But with lots of diplospeak, he admits that there are still some differences between different countries, and so he needs to “accelerate” the work on this issue.
On Ukraine , he says “Ukraine remains a central priority for Europe.”
“ Our resolve to stand by Ukraine and its people is as strong as ever. At the same time, every genuine step towards peace matters and we support all genuine efforts towards it. Unfortunately, Russia’s action point the other way.”
He calls for “more pressure on the aggression, not less,” and for more financial support for Ukraine, with a request to the commission to propose new funding.
“Supporting Ukraine has never been more relevant as Moscow is intensifying its hybrid campaigns across Europe. We see acts of sabotage and repeated violations of member states airspace in territorial waters.”
He talks about good discussions on cutting red tape and competitiveness of the bloc, and confirms what Merz said earlier about a special meeting on this issue in February next year ( 16:43 ).
On energy, he says that “current volatility on energy prices [is] putting intense pressure on households and businesses.”
“This crisis is triggered by events far beyond our borders, but we are also paying the price of our dependency on fossil fuels and the lack of a single market for energy. Given the urgency of the situation, there cannot be business as usual.”
He says there will be a united EU response to this issue.
He also talks about discussions on trade policy, with “total unity among the member states” on supporting the bloc’s efforts to diversify trade routes. Again, he references Merz’s proposals for a new trade instrument.
“Still someone here,” von der Leyen jokes as she gets to the podium.
I will bring you the key lines here, but you can watch along here:
Ursula von der Leyen and Antonio Costa hold news conference at EU summit - watch live

</details>

### 17. ★★★☆☆ US Stocks Set for Muted Open as Investors Assess Inflation Data and Technology Sector Volatility: Dow Jones, S&P, Nasdaq, Wall Street - Yahoo Finance

- **分类**: 📈 市场

- **多源确认**: 2
