- **发生了什么**: US Stocks Set for Muted Open as Investors Assess Inflation Data and Technology Sector Volatility: Dow Jones, S&amp;P, Nasdaq, Wall Street&nbsp;&nbsp;Yahoo Finance

<details>
<summary><strong>原文 / 抓取到的正文</strong></summary>

US Stocks Set for Muted Open as Investors Assess Inflation Data and Technology Sector Volatility: Dow Jones, S&P, Nasdaq, Wall Street
Dow Jones stock ticker ©Adobe Stock Images US stock futures pointed to a broadly unchanged opening on Friday as investors assessed recent market volatility, stronger-than-expected inflation figures and the outlook for technology shares.
Wall Street recovered sharply during Thursday's session after initially extending Wednesday's technology-led sell-off. The Nasdaq rebounded from its lowest intraday level in almost a month, while the Dow Jones Industrial Average reached its highest closing level in a month.
Despite the recovery, futures suggested a cautious start to Friday's trading, with investors continuing to monitor Treasury yields and expectations for Federal Reserve interest rate policy.
US equities experienced substantial fluctuations during the week, beginning with relatively subdued trading before technology shares came under pressure on Wednesday.
The selling continued into Thursday's opening session, pushing the Nasdaq to its lowest intraday level in nearly a month.
However, buying interest emerged as the session progressed, allowing all three major US indices to finish higher.
The Dow Jones Industrial Average climbed 556.21 points, or 1.1%, to close at 52,177.69, supported by gains in Nike (NYSE:NKE), Salesforce (NYSE:CRM) and Home Depot (NYSE:HD).
The S&P 500 advanced 25.12 points, or 0.3%, to 7,744.06.
The technology-heavy Nasdaq Composite recovered from earlier losses to finish 20.16 points, or 0.1%, higher at 26,713.03.
The rebound followed a period of selling pressure linked to renewed concerns about valuations and investment expectations in the artificial intelligence sector.
Federal Reserve Chair Highlights Economic Significance of AI
Technology stocks may receive some support from comments by Federal Reserve Chair Kevin Warsh during the annual meetings of the International Monetary Fund and World Bank in Bangkok.
Warsh described artificial intelligence as "the most profound economic transformation of a lifetime", highlighting its potential long-term implications for the US and global economies.
His remarks addressed the broader economic importance of AI at a time when investors have been reassessing the outlook for companies associated with the technology.
However, Warsh did not provide specific guidance on upcoming Federal Reserve interest rate decisions.
Investors therefore remain focused on economic data and market expectations for the central bank's next policy announcement.
Recent inflation figures have complicated the outlook for monetary policy.
The US Labor Department reported that its producer price index for final demand increased by 1.3% in September, following an upwardly revised 0.5% rise in August.
Economists had expected a monthly increase of 0.5%.
On an annual basis, producer price inflation accelerated to 6.3% from an upwardly revised 5.6% in August, exceeding market expectations.
Additional data released on Friday showed that US import and export prices also increased substantially more than economists had forecast in September.
Despite the stronger inflation readings, US stock futures remained relatively unchanged following the latest report.
According to CME Group's FedWatch Tool, market expectations continued to indicate that the Federal Reserve would leave interest rates unchanged at its forthcoming policy meeting later in October.
Thursday's recovery in US equities coincided with a sharp decline in Treasury yields.
The yield on the benchmark 10-year US Treasury note fell to its lowest level in three weeks, despite the stronger-than-expected producer price inflation figures.
Lower bond yields can support equity valuations by reducing borrowing costs and the discount rates applied to future corporate earnings.
The decline may have contributed to renewed buying interest following the earlier weakness in technology shares.
However, the persistence of inflationary pressures remains an important consideration for investors assessing the future direction of interest rates.
Gold, Airlines and Housing Stocks Lead Sector Recovery
Several sectors recorded substantial gains during Thursday's session.
Gold mining shares advanced alongside the price of the precious metal, lifting the NYSE Arca Gold Bugs Index by 4.7%.
The increase followed the index's decline to its lowest closing level in more than two months during the previous session.
Airline stocks also recovered, with the NYSE Arca Airline Index rising 3.8% after reaching a six-month closing low on Wednesday.
Housing-related equities posted strong gains, pushing the Philadelphia Housing Sector Index up 3.6%.
Biotechnology, healthcare and pharmaceutical stocks also attracted buying interest.
However, the recovery was not uniform across the market.
Computer hardware shares remained under pressure, with the NYSE Arca Computer Hardware Index declining 2.5% to its lowest closing level in well over a month.
Investors Await Direction Following Volatile Sessions
Friday's relatively flat futures suggested that investors were taking a more cautious approach after the sharp market movements of the previous two sessions.
Thursday's recovery demonstrated renewed buying interest across several sectors, although technology stocks continued to face uncertainty following the recent sell-off.
The stronger inflation data also raised questions about the outlook for US monetary policy, even as market expectations continued to favour unchanged interest rates at the Federal Reserve's next meeting.
Investors will be monitoring Treasury yields, technology sector performance and further economic developments for indications of the market's near-term direction.

</details>

### 19. ★★★☆☆ How Iran messaging highlights cost of war ahead of US midterm elections - Al Jazeera

- **分类**: 🌍 地缘政治
- **来源**: GN Geopolitics / Al Jazeera
- **多源确认**: 2
- **时间**: 2026-10-16T18:28:04+00:00
- **发生了什么**: How Iran messaging highlights cost of war ahead of US midterm elections&nbsp;&nbsp;Al Jazeera

<details>
<summary><strong>原文 / 抓取到的正文</strong></summary>

Share How Iran messaging highlights cost of war ahead of US midterm elections on social media
Gas prices are seen at a gas station in Deerfield, Ill., Wednesday, Oct. 14, 2026. [Nam Huh/AP]
In messages that appear to be directed at the American public, Iran has been highlighting the cost of the war that the United States and Israel launched against the country ahead of the United States midterm elections.
Iranian officials have published articles in state-affiliated outlets stressing the disastrous impact of the war on oil prices and the US economy, while Iran’s chief negotiator and Parliament Speaker Mohammad Bagher Ghalibaf has actively targeted US officials about rising costs and military spending.
list of 3 items list 1 of 3 Dozens of aircraft, hundreds of buildings: US loss to Iran attacks revealed
list 2 of 3 Iran war has cost the US $38bn: How will it impact US economy, politics?
list 3 of 3 What’s behind reports that the Israeli army is low on troops, equipment?
Sina Toossi, a senior non-resident fellow at the Center for International Policy, said Tehran is looking to increase the political cost of the war for Trump by trying to talk directly to the American public.
“It’s not necessarily that they’re trying to get Democrats in power or manipulate US politics in a certain direction, other than just overall increasing the costs on this issue of the Iran war, and just playing that game of information warfare and getting out their own narrative and propaganda as well,” Toossi told Al Jazeera.
Trump’s Republican Party is looking to retain control of the Senate and House of Representatives in next month’s midterm vote. But the Democratic Party, which largely opposes the war with Iran, is mounting a strong challenge across the congressional electoral map, including in areas that have been conservative strongholds for decades, such as Texas .
On Wednesday, Ghalibaf shared a news story on inflation rising to 3.7 percent last month along with a collage of negative headlines on the US economy.
The social media post also featured a comic strip from the Road Runner cartoon showing Wile E Coyote building a catapult that misfires and launches a boulder at him.
“Let’s ‘play the percentages’. Not in odds, but in the % increases,” Ghalibaf wrote in a message to US Treasury Secretary Scott Bessent, who has been leading the sanctions campaign against Iran.
Bessent said last month that he represents “the house”, likening the economy to a casino while addressing the national debt and bond market.
Iranian Parliament Speaker Mohammad Bagher Ghalibaf arrives at Zurich Airport in Switzerland, June 20, 2026 [Handout/Iranian Parliament Speaker Office/West Asia News Agency via Reuters] Ghalibaf’s remark referred to the increase in inflation, underscoring that the cost of living keeps rising due to the war despite Bessent’s assertions.
Days earlier, he shared an image of a ghost shaped like a bell curve, with diesel prices rising and housing affordability and consumer confidence falling.
Since the start of the war on February 28, Iran has disrupted shipping in the Strait of Hormuz, sending energy prices soaring. In the US, the price of diesel is at an all-time record of $6.33 per gallon (3.8 litres), according to the American Automobile Association, and gas is $4.33, up from less than $3 before the war.
Iran has intensified its attacks on vessels around the strait in recent days after Trump and his aides repeatedly stressed that the waterway is under US control.
The uptick in energy prices has fuelled overall inflation in the economy, leading the US Federal Reserve to raise interest rates last month at a time when Trump has been calling for them to be cut to stimulate economic activity.
In a letter to the American public on September 29, Iran’s Islamic Revolutionary Guard Corps (IRGC) accused Trump of lying about the cost of the war and the state of the US economy, dismissing the president’s claims that oil prices would soon nosedive.
“Countries around the world have lost their faith in the US economy. Demand for American bonds has plummeted. In fact, countries are starting to offload their US bonds. Soon, the dam will break, and the economy will implode, causing, unfortunately, great pain for Americans for many many years,” the IRGC said.
“Americans need to wake up. They need to wrest control back from corrupt politicians. Now is the time, as the American political system is at its weakest.”
The US midterm elections are seen as a turning point in the conflict – not because of their potential outcome, but because of what Trump might do after the vote.
Trump has suggested that he would either strike a deal with Iran or return to a full-scale war after the November 3 legislative elections. He has also dismissed the idea that the vote will have any impact on his Iran policy.
“They’re waiting to see how I do in the midterm election. What they don’t realise is that I’m not running,” Trump said at the United Nations General Assembly last month.
“I gave absolutely no credence and will not give credence to the election. When it comes to Iran, it doesn’t even enter my mind. The only thing that does is that Iran will never have a nuclear weapon.”
If the Democrats gain control of Congress, they can push for measures to curb Trump’s ability to continue the war, including through withholding funding for the military campaign.
But Toossi said there is a debate within Iran on the potential impact of a Democratic victory in November.
While some point to foreign policy hawks among the Democrats to argue that little would change, others say a midterm defeat would make him politically vulnerable and likely constrain his war powers .
“I don’t think they’re hedging their bets either way, and I don’t think it necessarily affects their overall strategy of increasing the costs [of war] regardless of whatever happens in November,” Toossi told Al Jazeera.

</details>

As

<details>
<summary><strong>原文 / 抓取到的正文</strong></summary>

A scene from NAZA, which interviewed military insiders anonymously on the rooftops of Tel Aviv at night. Photograph: NAZA/The Guardian
A scene from NAZA, which interviewed military insiders anonymously on the rooftops of Tel Aviv at night. Photograph: NAZA/The Guardian
‘Tough to watch, impossible to ignore’: reviewers on NAZA documentary
Film interviewing military insiders about mass killing of civilians in Gaza wins widespread praise around the world
Critics around the world have hailed the award-winning documentary NAZA as a “brilliant and harrowing” film that is “impossible to ignore”.
NAZA, which is produced by the Guardian and details Israel’s mass killing of civilians in Gaza, was released in UK cinemas this week and will be shown in more than 50 countries. As well as its global cinema releases, NAZA will be available free on the Guardian’s website from late November.
The investigative documentary, which contains 24 interviews with Israeli military insiders speaking anonymously on the rooftops of Tel Aviv at night, has received widespread praise.
In a five-star review, the Financial Times’s Danny Leigh says that as a piece of cinema, “journalism and film-making brilliantly entwine”. The Times calls the film “hugely provocative and a disturbing account” of Israel’s mass killing of civilians in Gaza, and the Irish Times brands NAZA an “essential record of engineered nightmare”. In a four-star review, the Guardian’s Peter Bradshaw says the “ anguish of Gaza is brought into pin-sharp focus ”.
British Vogue calls NAZA a “brilliant and harrowing film” that outlines the “restrained, meticulous, bone-chilling depiction of the progressive erasure of Gaza”. The magazine says the film “feels destined to become an enduring historical document, one which will and should be seen by generations to come”.
Time Out magazine says NAZA “puts together a restrained, intelligent, articulate, precise and utterly damning case against Israel’s actions in Gaza and their ongoing actions through the region. As a work of cinema, it is exemplary. As a work of journalism, it is inarguable. As a document, it is an indictment.”
The New York Times makes the film a “critic’s choice” and calls NAZA a “haunting” documentary that is “tough to watch, and impossible to ignore”. The Los Angeles Times says NAZA is a “stone-cold frightening” film that “burns the conscience”. And in New York magazine, it is described as “one of the most important films of our time”.
NAZA: the film Israel does not want you to see - video
The Hollywood Reporter praises NAZA as a “sober and thoroughly documented bombshell of an exposé” that “leaves one with a deadening feeling, as if humanity may have lost all hope”. The Toronto Star says “NAZA is heartbreaking but essential viewing, questioning the systems that cause and quantify civilian death in modern warfare”.
The Spanish outlet El Mundo hailed NAZA as “the film of our time, a superb, electrifying, and devastating documentary about the systematic model of annihilation of the Palestinian people” that ultimately “points the finger at us. At night, in silence.”
The French newspaper Le Monde says the film “has the effect of an uppercut that leaves the viewer stunned”, while the Danish culture magazine Soundvenue calls NAZA a “soul-crushing testimony to the genocide in Gaza” that is an “example of how the world increasingly seems like a dystopia”.
The United Arab Emirates’ state-owned newspaper the National calls NAZA a “sickeningly tough film to watch”, and an editorial in the Israeli-outlet Haaretz says the film is “trying to force Israelis to confront what they already know: that Israel’s conduct of the war in Gaza has left a black stain”.
When the film opened in Italy last month, it topped the charts . The Italian newspaper Corriere della Sera says NAZA lasts “only 80 minutes, but no more is needed: the ‘scientific’ coldness with which the interviewees reconstruct how they are destroying Gaza is something unforgettable”.
The film contains 24 interviews with Israeli military insiders who detail how an AI-assisted apparatus of mass surveillance was activated during the Gaza war, revealing the systems that were used to target Hamas operatives in the knowledge that large numbers of Palestinian civilians would also die.
Various organisations, including the UN, Amnesty International, B’Tselem and Physicians for Human Rights, the latter two of which are Israeli-based, have said Israel is committing genocide against Palestinians in Gaza.
The film premiered at the Venice film festival, where it was received a 25-minute standing ovation – a festival record. The documentary went on to win the special jury prize .

Fed Chair Kevin Warsh warns of AI 'shock' for economy, pledges to contain inflation
Warsh said AI would ultimately generate wealth but the path may be "very bumpy."
Federal Reserve Chair Kevin Warsh warned of a potential "shock" to the U.S. economy delivered by a rise in artificial intelligence spending , pledging to contain price increases as a flood of private sector funds drives up demand.
"If you’re a central banker, you’re in the business of trying to ensure stable prices and full employment, you’ve got to recognize this as a shock," Warsh said in Bangkok, Thailand, at a conference organized by the International Monetary Fund.
A surge in AI spending has helped boost the U.S. economy in recent quarters. In theory, an onrush of investment in activities like chip manufacturing and data center construction could put upward pressure in general on consumer prices, unless the demand is offset by a new supply of goods and services.
Which grocery prices are rising the fastest and which are falling?
"It’s obviously hitting the demand side of the economy before it’s hitting the supply side of the economy," Warsh said.
Warsh, who took the helm of the Fed in May, has previously weighed in on the potential impact of AI on the labor market and wider economy.
On Friday, Warsh reiterated his optimism about AI, saying the technology would ultimately generate added productivity and wealth.
But, Warsh added, "the path from where we are to there is likely to be a very bumpy one."
"We really don’t know what the market structure will be of these new technologies," Warsh said. "Who&#x27;s going to be making the money?"
Warsh acknowledged other shocks faced by the U.S. economy, including "shocks of war and military conflict."
The Nscale data center project in Barstow, Texas, Oct. 15, 2026.
The outbreak of the Iran war in February imposed a historic oil supply shock and caused an acceleration of price increases in many countries. A U.S. government report issued this week showed inflation had climbed to a 4-month high in September as the Middle East conflict continued to drive up gasoline prices.
Prices rose 3.7% in September, according to new data from the U.S. Bureau of Labor Statistics .

Inflation stands more than a percentage point higher than the Fed&#x27;s target rate of 2%.
"It’s hard to be in a discussion with central bankers and not compare notes and hear the same thing about the surge in prices," Warsh said on Friday.
He later added: "I’ve heard resolve from my colleagues around the world to deliver price stability. I’ve certainly delivered that resolve."
Price hikes prompted the Fed to raise interest rates a quarter of a percentage point in September, marking the central bank&#x27;s first rate increase since 2023.
An increase to the benchmark interest rate aims to slash inflation by slowing the economy and eating away at demand, though the policy typically yields results after a lag time lasting several months.
The next meeting of Fed policymakers takes place on Oct. 28, just days before the midterm elections.
Gas prices set record for highest number of days above $4 a gallon in a calendar year
Consistent with his approach in previous public appearances, Warsh opted against issuing a forecast about what the central bank may do at its meeting later this month.
"I don’t pre-judge meetings. I don’t believe in saying now what I might do at the meetings we have over the balance of this year," Warsh said.
Many investors appeared to interpret Warsh&#x27;s posture as a sign the Fed may hold interest rates steady later this month.
As of Friday morning, investors peg the chance of the Fed maintaining current interest rates at about 86%, which marks an uptick from about 55% on Wednesday, according to CME Group’s FedWatch Tool , a measure of market sentiment.

<summary><strong>原文 / 抓取到的正文</strong></summary>

CNBC Daily Open: Warsh holds cards close on Fed policy, but inflation numbers may tip his hand
Fed Chair Kevin Warsh said he does not “prejudge meetings.”
U.S. producer prices rose 6.3% annually in September, exceeding the 5.5% forecast
CoreWeave’s five-year credit-default swap spread briefly exceeded 1,140 basis points—a level associated with credit distress.
Twenty leading global AI and semiconductor stocks have lost more than $1 trillion in value this week.
Russia’s finance minister attended the G20 gathering in Bangkok, drawing resistance from European officials.
A television station broadcasts Kevin Warsh, chairman of the US Federal Reserve, speaking after a Federal Open Market Committee (FOMC) meeting as a trader works on the floor of the New York Stock Exchange (NYSE) in New York, US, on Wednesday, Sept. 16, 2026.
Hello, this is Hui Jie writing from Singapore, filling in for Leonie Kidd. Welcome to another edition of CNBC&#x27;s Daily Open.
Federal Reserve Chairman Kevin Warsh offered markets few clues in his final public appearance before the central bank&#x27;s October meeting. The latest inflation data, however, may have spoken for him.
Meanwhile, the AI trade had a rough Thursday as investors shifted their attention from what the technology can do to who will pay for it.
CoreWeave, whose debt is showing signs of distress, has become the sector&#x27;s latest cautionary tale.
In his last public appearance before the Federal Reserve&#x27;s October meeting, chairman Kevin Warsh gave no signals about where the central bank could go, saying he does not "prejudge meetings."
"I don&#x27;t believe in saying now what I might do at the meetings that we have in the balance of this year," Warsh said.
Speaking to the International Monetary Fund&#x27;s managing director, Kristalina Georgieva, in Bangkok on Friday, Warsh said he observed a "synchronous tightening in monetary policy in most places around the world," which meant there were no "free riders" in the global fight against price increases.
But economic data may provide a clue. U.S. producer prices rose 6.3% in September from a year earlier, hotter than the 5.5% forecast.
Earlier this week, consumer inflation also ran above expectations for September, rising to 3.7% from a year earlier, compared with a 3.6% forecast
AI stocks retreated on Thursday as investor worries over funding mounted, with so-called "neocloud" company CoreWeave front and center in the sell-off.
The company is facing mounting credit stress , with the yield to maturity on its 9.75% bond due in 2031 reaching 15.78%. Yields generally rise when investors view a bond as riskier.
Meanwhile, the spread on its five-year credit default swaps, contracts used to hedge against default, at one point soared above 1,140 basis points.
A spread level of 1,000 basis points or above is generally a sign of credit distress, said Gavan Nolan, executive director of fixed income pricing at S&P Global Market Intelligence.
Investors rotated out of parts of the AI trade amid mounting funding concerns, with Nvidia losing 1.84%, while AMD sank 1.5% and Intel lost about 2.8%. Dell Technologies sank 6.8%.
Collectively, 20 of the most valuable global AI and semiconductor stocks have shed more than $1 trillion in value so far this week, with higher interest rates, concerns about AI financing and intensifying competition weighing on sentiment.
Russia&#x27;s finance minister attended a Group of 20 meeting in Bangkok on Thursday, marking his second in-person appearance at the event since the 2022 invasion of Ukraine.
Anton Siluanov and Bank of Russia Deputy Governor Elizaveta Danilova were listed as Russia&#x27;s delegates at the finance ministers&#x27; and central bank governors&#x27; meetings on the sidelines of the IMF-World Bank Annual Meetings in Bangkok, Thailand, according to a seating plan seen by CNBC.
Siluanov was in the meeting, according to a person familiar with the matter.
The meeting also included German Finance Minister Lars Klingbeil, who had refused to take part a group photo with Siluanov at the August G20 finance ministers&#x27; meeting in the U.S. The photo was ultimately taken without the Russian delegation after European officials protested.
Klingbeil reportedly said after Thursday&#x27;s meeting that "there will be no pretending that Russia is now returning as a normal member of the G20 and simply taking its seat at the table again — not with me, and not with the other Europeans."
Hybe shares jump 10% as K-pop sensation BTS&#x27; tour lifts earnings hopes
Shares of Hybe, the company behind K-pop supergroup BTS, surged 10% on Friday as investors weighed an upbeat third-quarter earnings outlook fueled by its artists&#x27; global concerts.
Hybe&#x27;s third-quarter revenue is expected to reach 1.5 trillion won ($1.11 billion), up 106% from a year earlier, while operating profit is projected at 172.2 billion won, according to local brokerage firm iM Securities.
Concert revenue is expected to come in at 717 billion won, nearly tripling from a year earlier.

Fed&#x27;s Warsh withholds rate signal, praises recent global tightening
Fed Chair Kevin Warsh, in his speech Friday in Bangkok, declined to offer clues on whether there will be an interest rate hike again this month.
He acknowledged a "synchronous tightening" by central banks around the world, saying there are "no free riders."
He expressed optimism that artificial intelligence will make economies that use it well more productive and wealthier, warning the road there will be “very bumpy.”
Fed’s Warsh in Bangkok: &#x27;Not in that business anymore&#x27; of broadcasting decisions
BANGKOK — Federal Reserve Chairman Kevin Warsh refrained from signaling whether there will be another interest rate hike again this month, while acknowledging a "synchronous tightening" by central banks around the world.
"I don&#x27;t prejudge meetings. I don&#x27;t believe in saying now what I might do at the meetings that we have in the balance of this year," Warsh said, reiterating his opposition to the use of forward guidance for markets that should rely on interpreting data.
He also acknowledged that stance may disappoint anyone who had hoped for a steer ahead of the central bank meeting later this month. "It&#x27;s a new chapter at the Fed [and] we&#x27;re not in that business anymore," he said.
Warsh spoke at a fireside chat with the International Monetary Fund&#x27;s managing director, Kristalina Georgieva, as part of the weeklong IMF-World Bank Annual Meetings in Bangkok on Friday.
"Alongside other recent comments from FOMC participants, Warsh&#x27;s comments indicate that the FOMC is set to pause in October," Goldman Sachs economists led by Jan Hatzius wrote in a note. "We continue to expect a second and final hike in December."
S&P 500 and Nasdaq futures edged higher following the speech, while stock market indexes in Asia and Treasury yields were largely stable.
Chair of the Federal Reserve of the United States Kevin Warsh speaks during the International Monetary Fund (IMF) and World Bank Group annual meetings at the Queen Sirikit National Convention Center (QSNCC) in Bangkok, Thailand, on October 16, 2026.
Warsh described the global shift toward tighter monetary policy as a common fight on prices. Central banks made their own choices under their own mandates, he said, but the result was "a synchronous tightening in monetary policy in most places around the world," which means "there&#x27;s no free riders."
Surging energy costs stemming from the Iran war and artificial intelligence investment boom are stoking inflation and pushing up borrowing costs around the world, just as global public debt is on pace to exceed 100% of the world&#x27;s GDP, a level last breached in the aftermath of World War II.
The U.S. government has insisted that strong growth would be the best way to grow out of its rising debt pile, dismissing IMF&#x27;s warning that the plans of relying solely on growth won&#x27;t work as something that needs to be taken "with a grain of salt."
Warsh also expressed optimism about how AI will make economies that use it well more productive and wealthier, while warning the road there may be "very bumpy," and its near-term effect on employment is still uncertain.
"I want you to hear from me optimism, but optimism leavened by the seriousness of purpose," Warsh said.
A decade from now, he said, people will look back on the developments and "say this was hugely consequential."
The Fed raised rates to a range of 3.75% to 4% last month, its first hike since 2023 . Traders see a 72.3% chance for the Fed to keep the interest rate unchanged at the Oct. 27-28 meeting, according to CME FedWatch, while the majority expect a 25 basis point hike at the December meeting.

### 38. ★★★☆☆ Not Nvidia. Not TSMC. This Equipment Maker Is the Real Bottleneck in AI Chip Production. - Yahoo Finance

- **分类**: 🤖 AI/科技
- **来源**: GN AI
- **多源确认**: 1
- **时间**: 2026-10-16T19:15:00+00:00
- **发生了什么**: Not Nvidia. Not TSMC. This Equipment Maker Is the Real Bottleneck in AI Chip Production.&nbsp;&nbsp;Yahoo Finance

<details>
<summary><strong>原文 / 抓取到的正文</strong></summary>

Not Nvidia. Not TSMC. This Equipment Maker Is the Real Bottleneck in AI Chip Production.
Nvidia (NASDAQ: NVDA) and TSMC (NYSE: TSM) are often considered the bellwethers of the AI chip market. Nvidia , the world's largest producer of discrete GPUs for data centers, provides the best "picks and shovels" for the AI gold rush. TSMC , the world's largest and most advanced contract chipmaker, manufactures Nvidia's chips. It also serves other fabless AI chipmakers, including AMD (NASDAQ: AMD) and Broadcom (NASDAQ: AVGO).
Therefore, if Nvidia stopped developing new AI chips and TSMC stopped manufacturing them, the AI market would grind to a halt. However, neither company could produce new chips without ASML (NASDAQ: ASML) -- the real bottleneck in the AI supply chain.
Missed Nvidia in 2009? This Rare Signal Is Flashing Again. In 2009, a "Double Down" signal flashed for a little-known chipmaker called Nvidia. For the first time in years, that same "Total Conviction" signal is flashing for a company 1/100th the size of Nvidia. Continue »
Image source: Getty Images. Why is ASML the "real" bottleneck in the AI chip market?
ASML, which is based in the Netherlands, is the world's largest producer of lithography systems. These systems enable chip foundries, like TSMC, to optically etch circuit patterns onto silicon wafers. It dominates the market for lower-end deep-ultraviolet (DUV) lithography systems, which are used to produce older, larger chips. It's also the only producer of high-end extreme ultraviolet (EUV) systems, which are used to produce the world's smallest and densest chips.
ASML began developing its first EUV systems nearly three decades ago, giving it a major first-mover advantage against its industry peers. These massive systems cost between $200 million and $400 million, require multiple planes to ship, and must be assembled on-site.
All of the world's leading foundries -- including TSMC, Samsung, and Intel (NASDAQ: INTC) -- use ASML's EUV systems to manufacture their newest chips. Therefore, the latest AI chips from Nvidia, AMD, and Broadcom -- as well as high-bandwidth memory (HBM) chips for AI clusters -- couldn't exist without ASML's EUV machines.
ASML doesn't face any competitors in this market because it would cost too much to develop and mass-produce new EUV systems. That's why ASML still has plenty of room to raise its prices (and expand its gross margins) without worrying about losing any customers.
In the past, ASML generated more revenue from DUV systems than from EUV systems. But over the past three years, its EUV revenue has overtaken its DUV revenue as more foundries upgraded their systems to produce smaller, denser, and more power-efficient AI chips.
In the first half of 2026, ASML generated more than half of its revenue from its EUV systems (including its older "low-NA" systems and newer "high-NA" systems). That percentage should continue to rise as Nvidia and its peers launch even more advanced chips, and that growth should offset its declining DUV shipments -- which were exacerbated by tighter restrictions on its deliveries of higher-end DUV systems to Chinese chipmakers.
For 2026, ASML expects its revenue to rise 35%-38% with a gross margin of 56%. It's already raised that outlook twice in the second and third quarters of 2026, compared to its original guidance for 22% revenue growth with a gross margin of 51%-53% at the start of the year.
Analysts expect its revenue and EPS to rise 36% and 68%, respectively, for the full year. From 2026 to 2028, they expect its revenue and EPS to grow at CAGRs of 26% and 36%, respectively. Its stock still looks reasonably valued at 26 times next year's earnings, even though it's already rallied 177% over the past three years.
Nvidia and TSMC are still great AI chip plays, but they have clear weaknesses. Nvidia faces intense competition from AMD's cheaper GPUs, Broadcom's custom AI accelerators for hyperscalers, and other inference-oriented chipmakers. TSMC is constantly increasing its capex to ramp up its production and meet the market's insatiable demand for new AI chips.
So if you want a balanced way to invest in the AI market without betting on a single chipmaker or fretting over a contract chipmaker's rising capex, ASML checks all the right boxes. It controls a crucial link in the AI supply chain, is growing rapidly, and has a massive moat.

### 42. ★★★☆☆ Palestinians and US journalist injured by Israeli settlers in West Bank harvest attack

- **分类**: 🌍 地缘政治
- **来源**: BBC World / GN Top / NPR World
- **多源确认**: 3
- **时间**: 2026-10-16T14:31:04+00:00
- **发生了什么**: The BBC sees masked settlers attack Palestinians trying to harvest olives, amid a surge in similar incidents.

<details>
<summary><strong>原文 / 抓取到的正文</strong></summary>

Figure caption, Watch: Israeli settlers attack Palestinians in West Bank during olive harvest
By Lucy Williamson Middle East correspondent ,Â  Reporting from Abu Falah
Dozens of masked settlers have attacked Palestinians and set fire to cars and olive trees during the annual olive harvest in the occupied West Bank.
A BBC team witnessed one of several attacks in farmland near the Palestinian village of Abu Falah. A local official said the attacks had injured 12 Palestinians, including four dual Palestinian American citizens. A foreign journalist was also among those hurt.
Driving into the area early in the morning, we came across the first signs of the violence : a stream of cars carrying wounded Palestinian olive farmers racing out along the narrow stony track, piled high with olive picking equipment, as smoke rose from several fires nearby.
The bloodied face of Majdi Fheed peered out from the backseat of one car.
"We were getting our olive trees and almost 15 Jewish guys â¦ started attacking us with guns, with rocks, with special weapons. They hit me on my head [and] my neck â look, I am bleeding!"
Yaser AlKam, a council member from the neighbouring village of Turmus Aya told me Majdi - an American citizen - was attacked by settlers with nail-studded sticks, and needed treatment for deep wounds on his head. His elderly mother was also injured.
Another man ferrying the injured to hospital told us that settlers had attacked them with sticks while they harvested their olives, and had burned three cars â including one of his own.
"As soon as we got to the land and put our things on the ground, they started to come, so we ran away" said a woman next to him.
Image caption, Several Palestinian-American citizens and a foreign journalist were left injured following the attack
Asked about the violence, a US State Department spokesperson said they were aware of the reports.
"Weâ¯condemn criminalâ¯violenceâ¯by any partyâ¯in the West Bank and call for investigations, arrests, and convictions,â¯of those responsible," the spokesperson said.
Watching the vehicles stream past her, 73-year-old Imm Khaldoun told me emphatically that, no matter what happened, she would always return to her land. "This is my land. It&#x27;s my right [to be here]" she told me.
The UN says the past two weeks have already seen more attacks by violent Israeli settlers than during the entire harvest season last year.
Olive picking in the West Bank is now a test of speed, with trees rapidly stripped of fruit as soon as the sun rises, before violent Israeli settlers attack.
In another olive orchard nearby, we watched as dozens of farmers used power-tools to strip the trees as fast as they could â one eye on the ridge above us, where a group of settlers at a new illegal outpost had already gathered, and were watching the olive harvest below.
Standing on ladders, the Palestinian farmers worked quickly, sending streams of olives onto the plastic sheets laid out under the trees. Three villages had come together to harvest these olives, the landowner Amin Khalil, told me, as a form of protection against attack. He said his land was now surrounded by seven Jewish settlements and outposts.
Image caption, Yaser AlKam was left bloodied from the attack
All Israeli settlements are illegal under international law. Outposts â small encampments on hilltops â are illegal under Israeli law as well, but many still receive state support.
"This is a hotspot â there are seven settlements around here," Amin Khalil told me. "This year, all the people came together because when there are just a few people working alone at the edge of the village, the settlers attack. So all the families are working together today."
But the tactic doesn&#x27;t work today. As we watch, dozens of settlers, all dressed in black, begin moving down the hill towards us, setting fire to several groups of olive trees.
There are shouts of panic from the Palestinian farmers, yelling for children to get out of the field. Some begin swiftly wrapping up the olives to leave.
But with large numbers of people on both sides, the confrontation stalls into an uneasy stalemate. We see settlers with slingshots hurl stones towards us, and young Palestinian men running to throw stones in return.
As Israeli forces arrive, the standoff explodes into action. Settlers begin running down towards us as the farmers swiftly retreat. Some Palestinians set off fireworks to try and push the settlers back.
Image caption, The UN says settler attacks this year have so far killed one Palestinian and injured more than 120 others
American journalist Heidi Levine of the Washington Post was injured in the attack. She described how dozens of settlers ambushed her as she was reporting at the scene.
"I got jumped," she said. "I was on the ground and they were kicking me and I was trying to hold onto my camera and my bag. I had my helmet and flak jacket on which protected me. They made me stand up and they kicked me again, and this went on for at least 10 minutes."
In a statement, Washington Post executive editor Matt Murray said Levine had been clearly identifiable as a journalist.
"We are outraged by this unwarranted attack on a journalist doing her job and urge a thorough investigation and follow-through from authorities," he said.
The Israel Defense Forces (IDF) said personnel had been despatched to the area "following a report of a violent confrontation". It said a female journalist had been injured during the confrontation. And that "all circumstances" surrounding the incident would be examined.
As the farmers retreated, the sound of live ammunition cracked across the fields. It was unclear if settlers or Israeli forces were firing.
Minutes later, Israeli forces began firing tear gas into the Palestinian fields to end the confrontation â a well-worn Israeli pattern of managing these conflicts.
The UN says settler attacks on the olive harvest this year have so far killed one Palestinian and injured more than 120 others across the West Bank.
Illegal Jewish outposts have mushroomed here over the past few years, clustering in closer around Palestinian olive groves.
What we witnessed was part of a pattern being repeated day after day, as settler outposts here increase.
The yearly olive harvest has become a litmus test of how the policies of Israel&#x27;s fiercely pro-settlement government are affecting Palestinians here, by presiding over growing violence by extremist settlers, and fuelling Jewish claims to the land.

### 43. ★★★☆☆ China tells Israel to stop 'forced establishment' of West Bank settlements

- **分类**: 🌍 地缘政治
- **来源**: BBC World / GN China
- **多源确认**: 2
- **时间**: 2026-10-16T08:45:54+00:00
- **发生了什么**: Chinese Foreign Minister Wang Yi spoke to Israeli President Isaac Herzog on the sidelines of a conference.

<details>
<summary><strong>原文 / 抓取到的正文</strong></summary>

Image caption, China has been positioning itself as a mediator for conflicts in the Middle East
Chinese Foreign Minister Wang Yi has told Israel to stop its "forced establishment" of settlements in the West Bank and reverse its actions, his office said.
The "Palestinian question" is "the greatest historical injustice of this century", Wang told Israeli President Isaac Herzog in a face-to-face meeting on the sidelines of a conference in Azerbaijan on Thursday.
Beijing has been a long-standing supporter of the Palestinian cause and has maintained that a "two-state solution" is the "correct direction".
It has also been positioning itself as a mediator for conflicts in the Middle East.
In 2023 Beijing brokered a deal to restore diplomatic ties between Iran and Saudi Arabia after years of hostility. Earlier this year, it was part of efforts, along with Pakistan, to propose a ceasefire in the US-Israel war against Iran .
Beijing&#x27;s efforts to tackle the war in Gaza have focused on Palestinian unity.
In 2024 Beijing got rival Palestinian factions to agree to form an interim "national reconciliation government" for the occupied West Bank and Gaza after the war with Israel, although it was never implemented.
Now, Wang says the "immediate priority" is to fully open access to Gaza and stop the continued spread of a "humanitarian catastrophe".
China is willing to work with the international community to get the parties to return to dialogue and "seek a political solution to the conflict", he said.
In a post on X, Herzog said he was "pleased" to meet Wang and their talk "focused on the relations between our countries and the situation in the Middle East".
"I emphasised Israel&#x27;s desire for peace in the region, alongside the strategic threats to our security, first and foremost from Iran and its terror proxies," he wrote.
China maintains close ties with Iran and is the biggest buyer of Iranian oil, which had previously come under US sanctions because of Tehran&#x27;s nuclear programme.
China itself has been accused of trying to dilute its minority populations by encouraging Han Chinese migration to Tibet, which it annexed in the 1950s, and Xinjiang. Beijing rejects those claims, saying its policies promote economic development and social stability.
One year into the ceasefire, people in Gaza continue to live under the regular threat of Israeli strikes, displacement and food security challenges.
Although large-scale bombardment has stopped, Israel has continued strikes in Gaza almost every day . More than 1,400 Palestinians have been killed in Israeli strikes there in the year since the ceasefire started in October 2025, according to the territory&#x27;s Hamas-run health ministry, whose figures are considered reliable by the UN.
The US-brokered ceasefire was the first phase of a deal to end the war in Gaza, which had been triggered by a deadly Hamas-led attack on Israel on 7 October 2023.
But progress into the deal&#x27;s next phase has been stalled, with both Israel and Hamas accusing each other of violating the terms of their agreement.

### 48. ★★★☆☆ China’s Strategic Reassessment of India - War on the Rocks

- **分类**: 🌍 地缘政治
- **来源**: GN China
- **多源确认**: 1
- **时间**: 2026-10-16T07:33:03+00:00
- **发生了什么**: Join War on the Rocks and gain access to content trusted by policymakers, military leaders, and strategic thinkers worldwide.
As China and India have improved their relations in recent months, many observers have attributed this improvement to the end of U.S. strategic altruism toward India. Indeed, in the past year, China has watched India lose its balance and simmer in a sense of strategic disor

<details>
<summary><strong>原文 / 抓取到的正文</strong></summary>

Join War on the Rocks and gain access to content trusted by policymakers, military leaders, and strategic thinkers worldwide.
As China and India have improved their relations in recent months, many observers have attributed this improvement to the end of U.S. strategic altruism toward India. Indeed, in the past year, China has watched India lose its balance and simmer in a sense of strategic disorientation due to America's change of heart. Delegation after delegation of Indian strategists visited China, trying to mend ties in search of balance in the Chinese view. Many Chinese experts see India as returning to middle-power alignment for " economic authority and strategic freedom ," which translates into vigorous outreach to Europe, Japan, Canada, and all the middle powers. It's almost as if India is returning to its middle-power roots. The Chinese reassessment of India's power status and its strategic malleability &mdash; the extent to which its choices can be shaped &mdash; and predictability has significant implications for understanding future dynamics between the two powers. The renewed improvement in ties raises questions about its sincerity and limits. While China recalibrates its relations with India, it is also building fences and chokeholds, first and foremost through supply chain dependence. It is unclear what future policy the next U.S. administration will pursue toward either China or India. Both powers therefore keep their options open.
These assessments reflect ongoing research on China's role in South Asia. They draw on 10 research trips to China, as well as roundtables and exchanges held online and in Washington with Chinese experts since January 2025. China demonstrates a strong tendency toward binary judgments on India's alignment choice, while the reality is far more nuanced, as U.S. policy should be.
In the Chinese policy discourse on India, the first and foremost question has always been India's power status: Is India a great power, or is it simply a middle power with great-power potential? While India's gross domestic product has consistently been a fraction of China's, its claim to great-power status has had major sway over China's calculus. Vishwa Guru &mdash; "India the world's guru" &mdash; is a famous slogan created under the government of Prime Minister Narendra Modi that, in the Chinese view, best represents India's strategic aspiration: India not only aspires to be a world-class great power but also sees itself as superior and therefore will teach all the other great powers. Although Chinese policymakers do not have romantic misperceptions of India's actual power status, India's aspiration and, even more so, its boldness have been no small shock to Chinese policymakers, who follow the tradition of overestimating adversaries' capacity and hiding their own strength &#65288;&#26009;&#25932;&#20174;&#23485;&#65292;&#21028;&#24049;&#20174;&#20005;&#65289;. India's bold statement made Beijing question whether it underestimated fundamental aspects of Indian capability and potential that warranted India's great-power status.
More importantly, during the first Trump administration and the Biden administration, China stopped questioning India's great-power status. India played a pivotal role in America's Indo-Pacific strategy during these eight years, to the extent that Washington "hyped India's power status" &#65288;&#22823;&#32902;&#21561;&#25447;&#21360;&#24230;&#65289; and "exaggerated India's development potential."&#65288;&#22840;&#22823;&#20854;&#21457;&#23637;&#28508;&#21147;&#65289;
America's willingness to elevate India to a global great power and its seemingly endless provision of assistance to lift India up changed China's original view that only real comprehensive national power mattered. India's gross domestic product has been only about 20 percent of China's and has been unlikely to catch up any time soon to become China's peer. However, that could entirely change if American support continues. Beijing knows this too well because China had been the beneficiary of similar American support and elevation from the 1970s to the 2000s. The difference for China is that back then the United States supported China to counter the Soviet Union, and during the first Trump and Biden administrations, the United States lent similar support to India to counter China. The lesson for China is that great-power status does not actually have to be expressed in real, current terms rather than future expectations and potentials, and U.S. support could very realistically and rapidly elevate India to a new height and single-handedly make India a new great power.
Seeing India as an aspirational and rising power, China believes it followed a policy of accommodation with India throughout this period and beyond. General Secretary Xi Jinping continued outreach and friendly messaging to Modi, including in 2018 (right after the Doklam standoff in 2017); in 2024 at the Kazan summit of the expanded economic bloc originally formed by Brazil, Russia, India, China, and South Africa; and in 2025 when hosting Modi at the Shanghai Cooperation Organization summit. China did not pursue reciprocal, retaliatory economic policies toward India after 2020. Even on the issue of visas, Chinese officials cited the 280,000 visas China issued to Indian nationals in 2025 to highlight the 2,000 visas India issued to Chinese nationals during the same period to demonstrate the vastly different treatments. For China, these are all reflections of its unwillingness to completely sour ties with India.
But now, with India losing America's favor, India's great-power status has become much less persuasive. Chinese strategists are returning to the view that perhaps, after all, India is just a middle power. It has great potential, but elements in the Indian strategic culture pose irremovable obstacles and consistently sabotage its ascension.
India's Strategic Unmalleability and Predictability
As Chinese strategists observe the fluctuation of ties between the United States and India in the past nine years, it is unsettling and reassuring at the same time. The vast scope and depth of what the United States and India could jointly achieve materially and in practice is unsettling for China, exacerbating China's anxiety about India's future alignment or alliance with the United States and how it could undermine China's roles and aspirations. However, the limitations to what the United States could persuade India to do, even with great rewards and strategic benefits promised, are reassuring for Beijing.
For example, despite the favors and support the United States is willing to provide India, India has refused to abandon its strategic autonomy. At the height of the Russo-Ukrainian War and American demand for India to cooperate, India refused to abandon economic and energy ties with Russia. In private conversations, India's foreign policy community firmly distinguished between "like-minded" and "same-minded" countries to protect its strategic autonomy, and Indian diplomats vehemently emphasized issue-based alignments rather than permanent, rigid alliances.
For China, this is cognitively incomprehensible, as it essentially equates to "India wants to have its cake and eat it, too." If the United States does not have full confidence in Indian loyalty and support, why would the United States spend its valuable resources to do India the favor and let India run with its own agenda? If the answer is a shared U.S.-Indian aspiration to curtail China's rise and expansion, India's expressed reluctance to support the United States and confront China militarily on China's western flank in a future Taiwan contingency, a position that was reiterated amply in track two dialogues, has certainly made the case of "strategic altruism" less appealing for the United States.
On the drastically different interpretations of U.S. support and Indian reactions, all of the China and India specialists I spoke with refer to the Indian concept of " D&#257;n&#257; " (almsgiving) to explain what they see as India's "ungratefulness." What the United States identified as strategic altruism, India sees as strategic egoism because, in Indian culture, the concept of almsgiving views the recipient as doing a favor to the giver by providing them an opportunity to clear negative karma and practice detachment. Following this logic, Indian acceptance of U.S. assistance is India doing the United States a favor, and the United States should be in debt to India consequently. The correctness of this Chinese argument is debatable, but Chinese specialists' unanimous claim of this cultural reading of a religious tradition as the origin of Indian strategic behavior is certainly noteworthy.
Essentially, China perceives India's strategic choice as unmalleable, which significantly, or even critically, undercuts India's potential. Despite the amount of support the United States was willing to supply, India has adhered to its strategic autonomy and refuses to alienate Russia or support a U.S. military campaign against China. Similarly, despite the amount of accommodation China sees itself as willing to provide India, including in economic cooperation, India also adheres to its longstanding position and refuses to forge closer ties. That means India's strategic choice is predictable and cannot be shaped by the enticement of rewards or the threat of punishments. That substantially reduces the strategic value of India as a U.S. partner in strategic competition with China, and consequently, China's anxiety about India's alignment with the United States. Meanwhile, because of the expected limitation to India's cooperation with the U.S. on the latter's competition with China, it also considerably decreases India's strategic appeal for China to pursue.
Once the strategic questions of India's power status and its alignment choices are answered, China's remaining choices concern tactical approaches. With diplomatic engagement and maneuvering ongoing, China is most confident in the supply chain dependence it has built up vis-&agrave;-vis India. This is primarily prevalent in trade rather than investment, as India's stringent domestic investment environment and the restrictions on Chinese investment have made investment an unlikely candidate to boost bilateral relations.
Trade tells a very different story. It is in fact profitable for China, especially considering the overwhelming trade surplus China runs vis-&agrave;-vis India. In fiscal year 2025 to 2026, the total bilateral trade volume reached $151.1 billion, but India ran a $112.1 billion trade deficit. That means 87 percent of bilateral trade consisted of Chinese exports to India. The trade imbalance is therefore a prominent sore spot for India.
What China has identified, however, is India's middle position in the global supply chain. The argument is that India primarily imports from China materials, parts, and equipment that Indian companies use to make finished products for export to developed-country markets. The deficit India runs vis-&agrave;-vis China translates into a trade surplus it runs with other trading partners, such as the United States. India's economic dependence on China, therefore, is already established despite New Delhi's efforts to reduce supply chain risks associated with China. Decreasing India's dependence on China will create significant economic pain for Indian industries and companies. The same supply shock the United States experienced in 2025 in the tariff war with China will happen to India if New Delhi attempts to shut Chinese products out. This economic dependence alleviates China's anxiety over India's strategic autonomy and hostility, as it creates more leverage for China
The other distinct aspect of China's economic relations with India is the missing element of "empowerment," which is prevalent in China's ties with the rest of the South Asian subcontinent. "Empowerment" has been a pillar of China's Belt and Road Initiative engagement in developing countries, meaning China not only trades and invests in the recipient countries, but also utilizes trade, new productive forces, infrastructure development, and industrial cooperation to empower local economies from within. Examples include agricultural technology transfers in Nepal, green technology and the digital economy in Pakistan, and campaigns such as "Smart Bangladesh." However, "empowerment" is not an element in China's economic engagement with India, which raises the question of whether China deliberately avoids economic engagement that could elevate India's technological productivity, industrial upgrading, or high-tech development. One could say India rejects it from China anyway, but the lack of Chinese intention is obvious.
Predicting the future of Chinese-Indian relations is both easy and difficult. It is easy because bilateral relations have a ceiling, considering the many stressors from Pakistan to border disputes to regional competition to complicated geopolitical factors. It is hard because they could have no floor, given the potential for deterioration and the history of war, conflicts, and skirmishes between the two. For the time being, bilateral relations are stabilizing and improving, but their sustainability is subject to at least two unpredictable factors.
The first factor is Indian domestic politics , especially the relationship between the Rashtriya Swayamsevak Sangh (RSS) and the Bharatiya Janata Party (BJP) and the future successor to Modi. Despite the RSS being the parent and ideological guide of the ruling BJP, Chinese observers have noted growing tensions between Modi and the RSS due to electoral friction and the centralization of power and control by Modi. The recent trip by RSS chief Mohan Bhagwat to the United States is seen as a sign of the organization's growing desire to raise its profile. No matter who the next prime minister will be, that person is unlikely to command the vast popularity and persuasiveness Modi has enjoyed. As such, India's future internal cohesiveness and foreign policy appeal remain a question for China.
The bigger uncertainty in Chinese-Indian relations goes back to the United States. Washington's current India policy has taken a dramatic turn, but future shifts in the post-Trump era are also a high-probability event. There is no guarantee that the United States will not resume its competitive strategy vis-&agrave;-vis China and once again see India as a needed partner in the process. China believes that India is also waiting for the United States to change its approach again &mdash; therefore, nothing current is set in stone. The result is that neither China nor India will be willing to lock in their future strategic posture. Keeping options open remains the optimal approach, but that also means the current rewarming of ties may have an expiration date sooner than people realize.
These assessments have considerable implications for U.S. policy. If Chinese anxiety about the American-Indian relationship has genuinely diminished, then the deterrent value Washington derived from that relationship has diminished with it, which is a cost of the current policy that nobody is counting. If the Chinese read of Indian unmalleability is correct, then a post-Trump return to competition with China, and hence a return to cooperation with India, would face the same limits that frustrated the previous approach, and American planners should develop realistic expectations.
However, a more careful reading of the record also offers a more nuanced position on China's largely binary interpretation of Indian "malleability versus unmalleability." India by and large still holds significant skepticism and concern regarding China's intentions, capabilities, and influence. Despite U.S. dissatisfaction with India's lack of reciprocity, it would be erroneous to assume India's position has not moved at all, such as on defense procurement and the issue of Taiwan. The subtlety does not lie in whether things change, but to what degree. The takeaway is that the United States and India will have to resort to much more careful calibration and negotiation over the terms of their agreement, rather than throwing possible future cooperation entirely out of the window. In March 2026, Deputy Secretary of State Christopher Landau issued a blunt warning during the Raisina Dialogue that the United States would not repeat its "China mistake" with India. The delicacy, however, lies in the cost-benefit analysis, as most policy options are about the measurement of relative losses and gains, rather than an absolute, binary, black-or-white answer.
Yun Sun is the director of the China Program at the Stimson Center.
Image: Prime Minister's Office via Wikimedia Commons

### 49. ★★★☆☆ Is China’s housing market finally turning a corner? - Asia Property Awards

- **分类**: 🌍 地缘政治
- **来源**: GN China
- **多源确认**: 1
- **时间**: 2026-10-16T07:35:04+00:00
- **发生了什么**: Is China’s housing market finally turning a corner?&nbsp;&nbsp;Asia Property Awards

<details>
<summary><strong>原文 / 抓取到的正文</strong></summary>

Shanghai’s property revival raises hopes that China’s housing crisis is easing, but falling prices elsewhere point to a long and uneven recovery
When sales began at the new Greentown Shanghai Yuehaitang development in early August, the response exceeded expectations. Despite average prices CNY2,000 (USD300) per square metre above those of local competitors, all 228 units sold within days, with the project more than four times oversubscribed. Properties fetched an average of CNY62,198 per square metre, a local record.
The project’s distinctive above-ground, damp-resistant design and accompanying marketing campaign certainly helped, as did its prime location just 200 metres from a newly opened station on an extension of Metro Line 2. From Greentown Shanghai Yuehaitang, downtown Shanghai is a direct 35-minute subway ride away.
That a project so far from the centre of China’s most populous city sold out so quickly, and attracted such strong buyer interest, is one of several recent signs that a slow, uneven revival in the residential market may be underway. Shanghai is one of the few major Chinese cities showing signs of sustained price growth and buyer confidence as the country struggles to emerge from its worst housing crisis in a generation. Sponsored
But is the market finally bottoming out and beginning a sustained recovery, or is this just another false dawn?
Prices of new homes in Shanghai rose 3% year-on-year in July, more than in any other Chinese city, while prices for larger units of over 144 square metres climbed 5.5%, according to the latest data from China’s National Bureau of Statistics (NBS). Shanghai was also one of only five major cities to record month-on-month price growth for second-hand homes in July, with prices rising 0.3%.
Official data also showed that 147,000 second-hand homes changed hands in Shanghai during the first half of 2026, up 13% year-on-year.
“The price plus volume recovery together is different from previous false dawns,” says James Woo, head of valuations for China at Colliers. “The bottoming out is real but city-specific, not national.”
Beyond Shanghai, it remains difficult to argue that markets in most major cities have reached the bottom. Shenzhen has been the next-best performer, Woo says, with new-home prices climbing 7% in the first half of this year, second-hand home prices up 6.4% over successive months, and land premiums exceeding 150% in core districts.
Guangzhou is also “quietly stabilising”, posting the strongest month-on-month gains in the country’s second-hand market, at 0.4%.
Among Tier 2 cities, Hangzhou remains by far the strongest market, with new-home prices rising 2.6% year-on-year in July, the best performance after Shanghai, according to NBS data. Of the other cities surveyed, only Shenyang and Hefei, both up 0.3%, recorded annual price growth.
Nationally, therefore, just four of the 70 largest cities recorded rising new-home prices in the year to the end of July; the other 66 reported declines.
Yet after six years of market pain, signs that the market may finally be turning the corner have been greeted with both hope and caution.
In late May, a Reuters housing market poll found that respondents expected Chinese home prices to rise by an average of 0.3% in 2027, marking the poll’s first positive forecast for the following year.
Other analysts have since issued cautiously positive forecasts. “We expect home prices to drop at a more moderate pace in 2026 and bottom out by 2027-2028,” says UOB macroeconomic analyst Ho Woei Chen.
The Chinese government and state media have continued to strike a more positive tone, promoting central government measures and apparently seeking to restore confidence in the battered market.
In late April, the Politburo used stronger language than before in a policy statement, speaking of “striving to stabilise the real estate market”. It reiterated similar wording after its latest meeting in late July.
Earlier in July, Huang Yu, executive deputy president of the China Index Academy, spoke of “continued bottoming out and intensified differentiation”, acknowledging the increasingly varied pace of recovery expected across China for the rest of this year and beyond.
NBS Deputy Director Mao Shengyong told journalists in mid-July: “Data from the first half of the year shows that policies are gradually taking effect, and the real estate market has seen positive changes.”
Mao cited four consecutive months of price rises in Tier 1 cities before July and a “narrowing of the overall decline in residential property prices” in Tier 2 and Tier 3 cities.
Throughout much of 2026, China’s news media have promoted the idea that the market is bottoming out. State television network CCTV repeatedly referred to a xiaoyangchun, or “mini-boom”, following Chinese New Year in February.
Whether China’s residential market is turning the corner remains to be seen, particularly in smaller cities, Woo says.
“For 2026 as a whole, I’d expect Tier-1 city new home prices to approach year-on-year flattish [price trends] by year-end, with Shanghai clearly positive,” he says.
“The national picture will remain negative but with declining magnitude. The real question for 2027 is whether Tier-1 momentum can sustain without fresh policy stimulus, and whether Tier-2 cities like Hangzhou, Chengdu, and Suzhou can follow the Tier-1 lead.”

Broadcom Falls 4% as CEO Hock Tan’s Capital Warning Hangs Over AI Chips; Marvell Drops 4%
Broadcom (AVGO) fell 3% and Marvell (MRVL) dropped 4% after Hock Tan warned capital shortages could become AI's next binding constraint.
NVIDIA's general-purpose accelerators serve far more customers than AVGO or MRVL, limiting its exposure if large cloud buyers cut AI spending.
Multi-year custom chip contracts support Broadcom's bull case, but a capital crunch at concentrated cloud customers could push expected revenue into future years.
Just released. Our analysts combed the entire stock market and named the ten best stocks to buy right now, and Broadcom didn't make the cut. Enter your email to see the names that beat AVGO. The report is free. Enter your email and see if any of your stocks made the cut.
A blunt warning about AI funding from one of the chip industry's most prominent executives is hitting custom AI chip stocks harder than the wider semiconductor group. Broadcom ( NASDAQ:AVGO ) stock has dropped 4%, recently trading at $332.10. Meanwhile, Marvell Technology ( NASDAQ:MRVL ) stock is also down 4% and trades at $231.12.
Justin Sullivan / Getty Images Shares of Broadcom and Marvell are trailing the funds that track their corners of the market. The iShares Semiconductor ETF ( NASDAQ:SOXX ) is down 2.7%, a softer decline than either Broadcom stock or Marvell stock. Large-cap tech is holding up better (though it's still selling off), with the Invesco QQQ Trust ( NASDAQ:QQQ ) down 2.3%.
MRVL Price Target — 24/7 Wall St. Hock Tan Flags Capital as AI's Next Bottleneck
Broadcom CEO Hock Tan spoke at an event in Singapore. Earlier this week, he stated that investors "set very extreme expectations only to find out that it's almost like they are in a casino." Tan also warned that a shortage of capital could overtake chips and memory as the AI industry's next constraint. That second remark carries the most weight for Broadcom stock and Marvell stock, because it points at the money behind AI data centers.
24/7 Wall St has helped investors make money for over two decades, and our top analysts just finished ranking the definitive Top 10 Stocks To Buy Now . Not the ten biggest companies. Not the ten everyone is arguing about. The ten best stocks to buy right now.
The report is free, and you can see why we think each stock is a top investment today.
Broadcom and Marvell design custom AI chips for a small number of large cloud customers under multi-year programs. That structure gives Broadcom and Marvell long visibility into demand, and it also ties each to a short list of buyers, so even one of those customers pulling back on funding could hurt Broadcom and Marvell more than it would a supplier with a wider customer base.
NVIDIA ( NASDAQ:NVDA ) makes general-purpose AI accelerators that run across a wide range of customers and workloads. Its approach contrasts with the custom programs at Broadcom and Marvell, where each chip is designed around one buyer's specific needs.
The Invesco QQQ Trust counts Broadcom, Marvell and NVIDIA among its holdings, alongside a much wider mix of large-cap names outside the chip industry. Such a spread makes the Invesco QQQ Trust a broader gauge of large-cap tech than the iShares Semiconductor ETF, and memory, equipment and software names in the fund respond to different drivers than custom chip designers like Broadcom and Marvell do.
The bullish argument for Broadcom depends on custom chip programs that customers sign years in advance. Those commitments give Broadcom a revenue path that one conference remark doesn't change. Custom designs also take years to develop, which raises the cost for any customer that wants to replace Broadcom with another supplier.
On the bear side, the risk for Broadcom is that the capital shortage Tan described reaches the customers paying for those chips. Large cloud buyers facing reduced funding could stretch out deployment schedules, and a concentrated customer list leaves Broadcom with fewer ways to offset that. Even a modest delay could push revenue Broadcom expected in one year into the next.
AVGO Analyst Ratings — 24/7 Wall St. Capital Takes Center Stage in the AI Chip Trade
Tan's warning moves the AI chip debate from hardware supply to customer funding. Broadcom and Marvell now face the question of whether their large cloud customers keep financing AI buildouts at the pace those multi-year programs assume. Any sign that cloud buyers are cutting spending plans could pressure Broadcom stock and Marvell stock again.
Both stocks are falling harder than both funds. That is a sign the market is treating the capital warning as a specific risk for custom chip designers. For investors who share the bull case, a moderate position size makes sense, given the uncertainty Tan raised.

### 51. ★★★☆☆ The Stock Market Has Seen This Happen Only 2 Other Times in Over 150 Years, and History Has an Ominous Warning for Investors - The Motley Fool

- **分类**: 📈 市场
- **来源**: GN Markets
- **多源确认**: 1
- **时间**: 2026-10-16T17:39:46+00:00
- **发生了什么**: The Stock Market Has Seen This Happen Only 2 Other Times in Over 150 Years, and History Has an Ominous Warning for Investors&nbsp;&nbsp;The Motley Fool

<details>
<summary><strong>原文 / 抓取到的正文</strong></summary>

The stock market is entering the fifth year of the current bull market, which started in October 2022. During these past four years, the S&P 500 has surged nearly 120%, fueled by the artificial intelligence (AI) boom.
However, the question on many investors&#x27; minds is how much longer the good times can last. There are certainly signs the bull market could be losing steam. The breadth of market winners is actually quite narrow, with only a handful of stocks helping power the index. In fact, over a quarter of stocks in the S&P 500 ( ^GSPC -1.07% ) are actually down 40% or more from their all-time highs, while nearly 60% of S&P 500 stocks are below their 200-day moving average .
Meanwhile, renowned investors like Michael Burry, who correctly called the housing bubble, and billionaire hedge fund manager Ray Dalio have been predicting that the AI bubble will burst. Burry has been very vocal, shorting several popular AI stocks, including Nvidia , Micron Technology , and Palantir Technologies , while comparing the current market to before the 2000 dot-com bubble. Dalio, meanwhile, has said the AI boom shows classic signs of a bubble ready to burst.
One of the strongest indicators that the current bull market could be coming to an end is the cyclically adjusted price-to-earnings (CAPE) ratio . Created by Yale economist Robert Shiller in 1988, it measures the value of the S&P index relative to its 10-year average inflation-adjusted earnings. The purpose of this is to smooth out periods of earnings boom and bust cycles.
The valuation metric currently sits above 40, which is its second-highest level ever. The only two other times the CAPE ratio has moved above 32 were right before the Great Depression in 1929, when it reached 32.6, and during the dot-com bubble, when it peaked at 44.2. Both times the CAPE hit these levels, huge market corrections followed.
How investors should prepare for a potential market correction
Given the current warning signs in the market, it may seem like the common-sense thing to do would be to sell stocks and step to the sidelines. However, this would likely be a bad decision, as trying to time the stock market rarely works, and even if it does, then you&#x27;d have to correctly time getting back in as well. Miss out on some of the largest days in the stock market, and your investment returns could suffer significantly.
For example, Hartford Funds found that 76% of the market&#x27;s best days have happened during a bear market or the first two months of a bull market, and if you missed the 10 best days in the market over the past 30 years, your return would be cut in half.
Instead, I prefer to follow the advice of legendary investor Peter Lynch , who generated an astonishing 29.2% average annual return while running Fidelity&#x27;s flagship Magellan Fund. In a 1995 interview with Worth magazine, he said:
Far more money has been lost by investors preparing for corrections or trying to anticipate corrections than has been lost in corrections themselves. ... [i]n the long run it doesn&#x27;t matter much whether your timing is good or bad. What matters is that you stay invested in stocks.
In the same article, Lynch used the example that if you had invested $2,000 in the S&P 500 every year on Jan. 1 from 1965 until the time the article was written in 1995, you&#x27;d have an 11% yearly return. However, if you were unlucky enough to invest at the market peak each year, your return would only drop to 10.6%.
The lesson is ultimately that the best way to prepare for a potential market crash is to not prepare at all. You&#x27;ll be far better off in the long term consistently dollar-cost averaging into an index exchange-traded fund (ETF)  like the Vanguard S&P 500 ETF ( VOO -1.07% )  than trying to time a potential market crash.
